Image name: SEIS_task2_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are L and J.
2.  **Identify immediate connections from L:** Node L is directly connected to E and M.
3.  **Analyze path necessity:** Any path from L to J must begin by traversing either the edge L-E or the edge L-M. There are no other direct connections from L to any other node.
4.  **Consider removing a single node:**
    *   If E is removed, paths like L-M-J would still exist.
    *   If M is removed, paths like L-E-... (e.g., L-E-F-K-J) would still exist.
    *   Therefore, removing only one node (E or M) is not sufficient to disconnect L from J. This means the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
5.  **Consider removing two nodes:**
    *   If both E and M are removed:
        *   All paths starting with L-E are broken because E is removed.
        *   All paths starting with L-M are broken because M is removed.
    *   Since all paths from L to J must pass through either E or M as their first step, removing both E and M will effectively disconnect L from J.
6.  **Verify minimum:** We established that a single node removal is insufficient. We found a set of two nodes ([E, M]) that successfully disconnects L from J. Therefore, this is the minimum number of nodes.
7.  **Confirm nodes are not target nodes:** E and M are white nodes, so they can be removed.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, M]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['E', 'M']}

6.  **Level 5:** Identify neighbors of nodes from Level 4 (excluding previously visited nodes).

The shortest path found has a length of 5. Both paths `[L, E, B, H, I, J]` and `[L, E, B, C, I, J]` are valid and have the same minimal length. I will choose the first one.

*   E is directly connected to B. (Yes)
*   B is directly connected to H. (Yes)
*   H is directly connected to I. (Yes)

Image name: POGBb_task2_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are N and G. My task is to find the minimum number of non-target nodes to remove to disconnect N from G.

First, I will identify all possible paths from N to G.
N is connected to B, C, Y.
G is connected to H, K, X.

Let's analyze how paths from N can reach G:

**Paths starting from N:**

1.  **Paths through B:**
    *   N -> B -> H -> G (Path 1)
    *   N -> B -> E -> F -> K -> G (Path 2)
    *   N -> B -> E -> F -> X -> G (Path 3)
    *   N -> B -> E -> L -> M -> J -> D -> P -> I -> H -> G (Path 4)
    *   N -> B -> E -> M -> J -> D -> P -> I -> H -> G (Path 5)

2.  **Paths through C:**
    *   N -> C -> I -> H -> G (Path 6)
    *   N -> C -> P -> I -> H -> G (Path 7)
    *   N -> C -> O -> P -> I -> H -> G (Path 8)
    *   N -> C -> P -> D -> J -> M -> E -> F -> K -> G (Path 9)
    *   N -> C -> P -> D -> J -> M -> E -> F -> X -> G (Path 10)
    *   N -> C -> O -> P -> D -> J -> M -> E -> F -> K -> G (Path 11)
    *   N -> C -> O -> P -> D -> J -> M -> E -> F -> X -> G (Path 12)

3.  **Paths through Y:**
    *   N -> Y -> O -> P -> I -> H -> G (Path 13)
    *   N -> Y -> O -> P -> D -> J -> M -> E -> F -> K -> G (Path 14)
    *   N -> Y -> O -> P -> D -> J -> M -> E -> F -> X -> G (Path 15)

**Analyzing the "exit points" to G:**
All paths to G must pass through one of its direct neighbors: H, K, or X.

Let's examine how K and X are reached:
*   K is connected to F and G. To reach K from N, a path must go through F.
*   X is connected to F and G. To reach X from N, a path must go through F.
*   F is connected to E, K, X. To reach F from N, a path must go through E.

This means that any path from N to G that goes through K or X must necessarily pass through node E (N -> ... -> E -> F -> K/X -> G).

Therefore, all paths from N to G can be categorized into two groups:
1.  Paths that reach G via H.
2.  Paths that reach G via K or X, which means they must pass through E.

So, every single path from N to G must pass through either node H or node E.

**Testing potential cut sets:**

*   **Can a single node disconnect N and G?**
    *   If we remove H, paths through E (e.g., N -> B -> E -> F -> K -> G) would still exist.
    *   If we remove E, paths through H (e.g., N -> B -> H -> G) would still exist.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

*   **Can two nodes disconnect N and G?**
    *   Based on the analysis above, if we remove both H and E, all paths from N to G will be broken.
        *   Any path that would have gone through H is broken by removing H.
        *   Any path that would have gone through E (to reach K or X) is broken by removing E.
        *   Since all paths must go through either H or E, removing both H and E guarantees disconnection.

**Verification of the cut set [H, E]:**
1.  **Paths through H:** All paths that use H (e.g., N -> B -> H -> G, N -> C -> I -> H -> G, N -> Y -> O -> P -> I -> H -> G) are broken by removing H.
2.  **Paths through E:** All paths that use E (e.g., N -> B -> E -> F -> K -> G, N -> C -> P -> D -> J -> M -> E -> F -> X -> G, N -> Y -> O -> P -> D -> J -> M -> E -> F -> K -> G) are broken by removing E.

Since every path from N to G must pass through at least one of H or E, removing both H and E will disconnect N from G. This is a cut set of size 2. As we've established that a single node is not enough, 2 is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['E', 'H']}
Progress: 4/280 images processed.

Image name: PG_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are J and F. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect J and F. I cannot remove J or F.

First, I will identify all direct neighbors of J and F to understand the immediate connections.
Neighbors of J: D, P, M, I
Neighbors of F: E, K, X, G, B

Next, I will try to find vertex-disjoint paths between J and F. Finding the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths will give a lower bound for the minimum vertex cut, according to Menger's Theorem.

Let's identify some vertex-disjoint paths (excluding J and F themselves):
1.  **Path 1:** J - M - L - E - F
    Intermediate nodes: {M, L, E}
2.  **Path 2:** J - I - H - G - F
    Intermediate nodes: {I, H, G}
3.  **Path 3:** J - P - O - N - B - F
    Intermediate nodes: {P, O, N, B}

Let's check if these three paths are indeed vertex-disjoint in their intermediate nodes:
-   Intermediate nodes for Path 1: {M, L, E}
-   Intermediate nodes for Path 2: {I, H, G}
-   Intermediate nodes for Path 3: {P, O, N, B}
All sets of intermediate nodes are distinct. Therefore, these three paths are vertex-disjoint.

Since there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between J and F, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I will attempt to find a cut set of size 3. A natural choice for a cut set would be to remove one node from each of these vertex-disjoint paths.
Consider the nodes M, I, and P. These are all direct neighbors of J (or D, which is only connected to P from J's side).

Let's verify if removing the nodes {M, I, P} disconnects J from F:
-   **Removing M:** This breaks all paths that start with J -> M. For example, Path 1 (J - M - L - E - F) is broken.
-   **Removing I:** This breaks all paths that start with J -> I. For example, Path 2 (J - I - H - G - F) is broken.
-   **Removing P:** This breaks all paths that start with J -> P. For example, Path 3 (J - P - O - N - B - F) is broken. Additionally, node D is a neighbor of J, but D is only connected to P (and J). Since P is removed, any path starting J -> D -> P... is also broken.

By removing M, I, and P, all immediate connections from J to the rest of the graph (via M, I, P, or D which leads to P) are severed. Therefore, J becomes completely disconnected from F.

Since we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. We have also found a cut set of size 3 ({M, I, P}) that successfully disconnects J and F. Thus, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, I, P]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['E', 'H']}

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are labeled X and N. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node X to find the shortest path to node N.

First, I list the direct connections for each node to build an adjacency list, carefully verifying each line:
*   **X (black node):** K, G, F
*   **N (black node):** B, C, O, Y
*   K: X, G, F
*   G: X, K, F, H
*   F: X, K, G, E
*   H: G, E, B, I
*   E: F, H, L, M
*   B: H, C, N
*   I: H, C, P, J
*   C: B, I, N, O
*   O: N, C, P, Y
*   Y: N, O
*   L: E, M
*   M: E, L, J
*   J: I, M, P, D
*   P: I, J, D, C, O
*   D: J, P

Now, I start the BFS from X:

*   **Level 0:**
    *   Current node: X. Path: [X].
    *   Queue: [(X, [X])]
    *   Visited: {X}

*   **Level 1 (Length 1):**
    *   Pop (X, [X]) from queue.
    *   Neighbors of X: K, G, F.
        *   Add (K, [X, K]) to queue. Visited: {X, K}
        *   Add (G, [X, G]) to queue. Visited: {X, K, G}
        *   Add (F, [X, F]) to queue. Visited: {X, K, G, F}
    *   Queue: [(K, [X, K]), (G, [X, G]), (F, [X, F])]

*   **Level 2 (Length 2):**
    *   Pop (K, [X, K]). Neighbors: X (visited), G (visited), F (visited). No new unvisited nodes.
    *   Pop (G, [X, G]). Neighbors: X (visited), K (visited), F (visited), H.
        *   Add (H, [X, G, H]) to queue. Visited: {X, K, G, F, H}

2.  **Analyze Connectivity of Target Node D:**
    *   Node D is directly connected to two other nodes: J and P.
    *   The edges connecting D to the rest of the graph are (D, J) and (D, P).

3.  **Consider Removing One Edge:**
    *   If we remove only the edge (D, J), there are still paths from E to D through P (e.g., E-B-C-I-P-D).
    *   If we remove only the edge (D, P), there are still paths from E to D through J (e.g., E-M-J-D).
    *   Due to the high connectivity of the graph, removing any single edge elsewhere (e.g., (E, B), (M, J), (I, P)) would still leave multiple alternative paths between E and D.
    *   Therefore, it is impossible to disconnect E and D by removing only one edge. The minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

4.  **Consider Removing Two Edges:**

    *   If we remove both edges connected to D, namely (D, J) and (D, P), node D becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    *   With D isolated, no path can exist between E and D.

    *   **Path 1:** E - G - H - I (Intermediate nodes: G, H)
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - C - I (Intermediate nodes: B, C)

    *   Nodes in Path 2: {B, C}
    *   Nodes in Path 3: {M, J}

3.  **Propose a cut set of size 3:** Since the minimum cut size is at least 3, I will try to find a cut set of size 3. A common strategy is to pick one node from each of the node-disjoint paths identified, especially nodes close to the source or sink.
    Let's consider the set of nodes {G, B, M}. These are the first intermediate nodes in each of the three node-disjoint paths identified above.

    *   Node N is connected to B, C, O, Y.

    *   To disconnect N, I could remove all its incident edges: (N, B), (N, C), (N, O), (N, Y). This is a cut of size 4.
    *   Since a cut of size 3 is possible, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):**
    To determine if a cut of size 2 is possible, I need to check if there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and N. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least 3.

    Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths from M to N:

    *   **Path 1:** M - E - H - B - N
        Edges: {(M, E), (E, H), (H, B), (B, N)}

    *   **Path 2:** M - J - I - C - N
        Edges: {(M, J), (J, I), (I, C), (C, N)}

        Edges: {(M, L), (L, E), (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, O), (O, N)}

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
    *   **Edges from Path 1:** (M,E), (E,H), (H,B), (B,N)
    *   **Edges from Path 2:** (M,J), (J,I), (I,C), (C,N)
    *   **Edges from Path 3:** (M,L), (L,E), (E,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,P), (P,O), (O,N)

    By inspecting the edge sets, it is clear that no edge is shared between any two of these three paths. For example:
    *   Path 1 uses (M,E), Path 2 uses (M,J), Path 3 uses (M,L) - all distinct starting edges from M.
    *   Path 1 ends with (B,N), Path 2 ends with (C,N), Path 3 ends with (O,N) - all distinct ending edges to N.
    *   All intermediate edges are also distinct across the three paths.

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and N, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect M and N is at least 3.

4.  **Identify a minimal cut set of size 3:**
    Since we know the minimum is 3, we can now propose a cut set of 3 edges.
    One such cut set is to remove all edges directly connecting to N, except for (N,Y) which is not part of the main flow of paths from M.
    Consider the edges: (N, B), (N, C), (N, O).

    Let's verify this cut set:

    *   Removing (N, C) breaks Path 2 (M-J-I-C-N) and any other path ending at N via C.
    *   Removing (N, O) breaks Path 3 (M-L-E-G-H-I-P-O-N) and any other path ending at N via O.
    *   Node Y is connected to N and O. If (N,O) is removed, then any path M-...-O-Y-N is also broken.
    *   With these three edges removed, N is completely isolated from any path originating from M.

    Another valid cut set of size 3 would be to remove all edges directly connected to M: [(M, E), (M, L), (M, J)]. This would also disconnect M from N.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   Any path starting F-E-... will be broken if the edge (F, E) is removed.
    *   Any path starting F-G-... will be broken if the edge (F, G) is removed.

    *   Any path starting F-X-... must eventually go through G (e.g., F-X-G-...) to reach J, as X is only connected to F, K, G. If (F, G) is removed, X cannot reach G.

    If we remove the edge (F, E), all paths that start with F-E are broken.
    If we remove the edge (F, G), all paths that start with F-G are broken. Furthermore, since K and X are only connected to F and G (and each other), removing (F, G) effectively isolates the cluster {F, K, X} from the rest of the graph. This means no path from F (via K or X) can reach J.

5.  **Verify the cut set:**
    By removing both (F, E) and (F, G), F becomes completely disconnected from the rest of the graph that leads to J. The only remaining connections for F are to K and X, but K and X themselves cannot reach J without passing through G (which is now disconnected from F, K, X) or E (which is disconnected from F). Therefore, no path exists between F and J.

6.  **Check for minimality:**
    If only (F, E) is removed, paths through (F, G) still exist (e.g., F-G-H-I-J).
    If only (F, G) is removed, paths through (F, E) still exist (e.g., F-E-M-J).
    Therefore, both edges are necessary to achieve complete disconnection, making the cut set minimal.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

    *   Visited: `{}`

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of E and J to understand the immediate connections:
*   Neighbors of E: L, M, F, B
*   Neighbors of J: D, K, I, P

I will analyze the paths from E to J by grouping them based on the first edge taken from E.

**Group 1: Paths starting from E through L, M, or F.**
*   Any path starting E-L... must go through M (E-L-M...).
*   Any path starting E-M... must go through F (E-M-F...) or L (E-M-L...).
*   Any path starting E-F...
*   From nodes L, M, F, the only way to reach J is through the sub-graph containing F, G, X, K. All paths from F, G, X eventually converge at K before reaching J.
    *   Examples: E-F-K-J, E-F-G-K-J, E-F-X-G-K-J, E-M-F-K-J, E-L-M-F-K-J, etc.
*   Therefore, the edge **(K, J)** is a critical edge for all paths originating from E and passing through L, M, or F. If (K, J) is removed, all these paths are broken.

**Group 2: Paths starting from E through B.**
*   Any path starting E-B...
*   From node B, paths can go through H, N, or C. These paths eventually lead to I or P, which then connect to J.
    *   Examples: E-B-H-I-J, E-B-N-C-I-J, E-B-N-O-P-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-C-O-P-J.
*   Therefore, the edge **(E, B)** is a critical edge for all paths originating from E and passing through B. If (E, B) is removed, all these paths are broken.

**Proposed Cut Set:**
Based on the analysis above, if I remove the edge (K, J), all paths from E that go through L, M, or F are disconnected from J.
If I also remove the edge (E, B), all paths from E that go through B are disconnected from J.

Let's verify this cut set: **[(E, B), (K, J)]**.
1.  **Removing (E, B):** This immediately severs all connections from E to the "lower" part of the graph (B, H, N, C, I, O, P). No path from E can reach J via this route.
2.  **Removing (K, J):** After (E, B) is removed, E can only reach L, M, or F.
    *   From L, the only path is L-M-F...
    *   From M, the only path is M-F...
    *   From F, paths can go to K, G, or X.
    *   G is connected to K and X. X is connected to K and G.
    *   Ultimately, all paths from F, G, X must pass through K to reach J.
    *   Since (K, J) is removed, no path from F, G, X, and consequently from L or M, can reach J.

Since all possible paths from E to J are broken by removing these two edges, this is a valid cut set. The size of this cut set is 2.

**Minimality Check:**
Could there be a cut set of size 1?
*   If I remove only (E, B), paths like E-F-K-J would still exist.
*   If I remove only (K, J), paths like E-B-H-I-J would still exist.
*   There is no single edge whose removal would disconnect E and J, as they are connected by multiple independent paths.
Therefore, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.

Since a cut set of size 1 is not possible, and I have found a cut set of size 2, this must be the minimum number of edges to remove.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, B), (K, J)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['G', 'H']], [['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['F', 'E']]]}

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are O (black) and H (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect O from H.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node O is connected to: P, Y, N, M.
    *   Node H is connected to: I, C, X, G.

2.  **Conceptualize the graph into two main "sides" or partitions:**
    Let's consider a partition of the graph into two sets of nodes, S and T, such that O is in S and H is in T. The minimum cut will be the smallest set of edges that connect a node in S to a node in T.

    Let's try to define a set S containing O and all nodes that can be reached from O without passing through any "bottleneck" edges leading towards H.
    Let S = {O, M, L, E, B, N, Y, P, D, J}. These are the nodes that are "on the O-side" of the graph.
    Let T = {H, I, C, X, G, K, F}. These are the nodes that are "on the H-side" of the graph.

3.  **Identify all edges that cross this partition (i.e., connect a node in S to a node in T):**
    *   **(J, I)**: J is in S, I is in T. This edge connects the two partitions.
    *   **(N, C)**: N is in S, C is in T. This edge connects the two partitions.
    *   **(B, C)**: B is in S, C is in T. This edge connects the two partitions.
    *   **(E, F)**: E is in S, F is in T. This edge connects the two partitions.

    Let's verify that no other edges connect S to T:
    *   Edges within S: (O,P), (O,Y), (O,N), (O,M), (P,D), (P,Y), (Y,N), (M,J), (M,L), (J,D), (N,B), (B,E), (E,L). All these edges connect two nodes within S.
    *   Edges within T: (I,C), (I,H), (C,H), (X,H), (G,H), (F,X), (F,K), (K,X), (K,G), (X,G). All these edges connect two nodes within T.

    The set of edges crossing this partition is E_cut = {(J,I), (N,C), (B,C), (E,F)}. The size of this set is 4.

4.  **Verify that removing these 4 edges disconnects O from H:**
    *   Removing (J,I) breaks all paths from O to I that go through J (e.g., O-M-J-I-H, O-P-D-J-I-H). Node I has no other connections to the S-side.
    *   Removing (N,C) and (B,C) breaks all paths from O to C that go through N or B (e.g., O-P-Y-N-C-H, O-N-B-C-H, O-M-L-E-B-C-H). Node C has no other connections to the S-side.
    *   Removing (E,F) breaks all paths from O to F. Since all paths from O to X or G must pass through F (or K, which is connected to F), and all paths to F or K from O must pass through E, cutting (E,F) effectively isolates F, K, X, and G from O. This breaks paths like O-N-B-E-F-X-H, O-M-L-E-F-K-G-H, etc.

    Therefore, removing these 4 edges completely disconnects O from H.

5.  **Prove that 3 edges are not sufficient (i.e., this is the minimum cut):**
    To prove that 4 is the minimum, we need to show that if any 3 of these 4 edges are removed, there still exists at least one path between O and H.
    *   If we remove {(J,I), (N,C), (B,C)}, the edge (E,F) remains. A path like O-N-B-E-F-X-H still exists.
    *   If we remove {(J,I), (N,C), (E,F)}, the edge (B,C) remains. A path like O-N-B-C-H still exists.

    *   If we remove {(N,C), (B,C), (E,F)}, the edge (J,I) remains. A path like O-M-J-I-H still exists.

    Since removing any 3 of these 4 edges leaves at least one path between O and H, all 4 edges are necessary to achieve disconnection.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are J (black) and E (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between J and E.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node J is connected to: D, P, M, I.
    *   Node E is connected to: B, F, L.

2.  **Analyze all possible paths from J to E:**
    Any path from J to E must eventually pass through one of E's direct neighbors: B, F, or L.
    Let's list some representative paths to illustrate the different routes:
    *   **Paths through L:**
        *   J - M - L - E
    *   **Paths through B:**
        *   J - D - P - O - N - B - E
        *   J - D - P - O - C - B - E
        *   J - P - O - N - B - E
        *   J - P - O - C - B - E
        *   J - M - O - N - B - E
        *   J - M - O - C - B - E
        *   J - I - C - B - E
        *   J - I - H - C - B - E
    *   **Paths through F:**
        *   J - I - H - X - K - F - E
        *   J - I - H - G - K - F - E
        *   J - I - G - K - F - E
        *   J - I - G - X - K - F - E
        *   J - D - P - O - N - B - F - E (Note: B is connected to F)
        *   J - D - P - O - C - B - F - E

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to cut:**
    To disconnect J from E, we must break all paths between them. Since all paths to E must pass through B, F, or L, we need to ensure that no path can reach B, F, or L from J, or that B, F, and L cannot reach E.

    *   **Consider cutting edges directly connected to E:**
        The edges directly connected to E are (B, E), (F, E), and (L, E).
        If we remove all three of these edges:
        1.  (B, E) is removed: No path can reach E via node B.
        2.  (F, E) is removed: No path can reach E via node F.
        3.  (L, E) is removed: No path can reach E via node L.
        By removing these three edges, E becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from J. This is a cut set of size 3: `[(B, E), (F, E), (L, E)]`.

    *   **Check if a cut of size 1 or 2 is possible:**
        *   **Size 1:** If only one edge is removed (e.g., (B, E)), paths through F and L would still exist (e.g., J-M-L-E and J-I-G-K-F-E). So, 1 edge is not enough.
        *   **Size 2:** If two edges are removed (e.g., (B, E) and (F, E)), paths through L would still exist (e.g., J-M-L-E). So, 2 edges are not enough. This applies regardless of which two edges are chosen from `{(B, E), (F, E), (L, E)}`.
        *   What if we choose edges not directly connected to E? For example, if we remove (M, L) and (N, B). Paths like J-I-G-K-F-E would still exist, and paths like J-I-C-B-E would still exist (since (C, B) is not removed). This confirms that 2 edges are insufficient.

    Since 2 edges are not enough, and we found a valid cut set of 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:

6.  **Verify minimality:** We've established that a single node removal is insufficient. A set of two nodes, [J, O], successfully disconnects P from N. Therefore, 2 is the minimum number of nodes.

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

Let's identify edge-disjoint paths between E and J:

1.  **Path 1: E - M - J**
    Edges: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`

2.  **Path 2: E - B - C - I - J**
    Edges: `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, J)`
    This path does not share any edges with Path 1.

3.  **Path 3: E - F - K - G - H - B - N - Y - O - P - J**
    Edges: `(E, F)`, `(F, K)`, `(K, G)`, `(G, H)`, `(H, B)`, `(B, N)`, `(N, Y)`, `(Y, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, J)`
    Let's verify if this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
    *   Compared to Path 1 `{(E, M), (M, J)}`: No common edges.
    *   Compared to Path 2 `{(E, B), (B, C), (C, I), (I, J)}`:
        *   `(E, F)` vs `(E, B)`: Different.
        *   `(F, K)` vs `(B, C)`: Different.
        *   `(K, G)` vs `(C, I)`: Different.
        *   `(G, H)` vs `(I, J)`: Different.
        *   `(H, B)` vs `(B, C)`: Different.
        *   `(B, N)` vs `(B, C)`: Different.
        *   The remaining edges of Path 3 are also distinct from Path 2's edges.
    Therefore, Path 3 is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects E and J.
A straightforward approach is to consider all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes and identify a minimal subset that cuts all paths.

Let's consider the edges directly connected to J:
*   `(M, J)`
*   `(D, J)`
*   `(P, J)`
*   `(I, J)`

If we remove the edges `(M, J)`, `(P, J)`, and `(I, J)`:
1.  Path 1 (E - M - J) is broken by removing `(M, J)`.
2.  Path 2 (E - B - C - I - J) is broken by removing `(I, J)`.
3.  Path 3 (E - F - K - G - H - B - N - Y - O - P - J) is broken by removing `(P, J)`.

After removing these three edges, the only remaining connection to J is `(D, J)`.
Node D is connected only to J and P. Since `(P, J)` has been removed, D is now only connected to J. There is no path from E to D (and subsequently to J) that does not involve J itself or the removed edges. Therefore, J is completely disconnected from E.

This cut set `[(M, J), (P, J), (I, J)]` has 3 edges, which matches the minimum number determined by Menger's theorem.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (P, J), (I, J)]

Image name: PGS_task3_gt2_PAIR4

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are G and N.
2.  **Goal:** Determine the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

3.  **Analyze Connections to Node N:**
    Node N is directly connected to four other nodes: B, C, Y, and O.
    The edges connecting N to the rest of the graph are: (N, B), (N, C), (N, Y), and (N, O).

4.  **Consider a Cut Set of Size 4:**
    If we remove all four edges directly connected to N, i.e., [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y), (N, O)], then node N becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between G and N. This is a valid cut set of size 4.

5.  **Check for a Smaller Cut (Size 3):**
    To confirm that 4 is the *minimum* number, we must demonstrate that it's impossible to disconnect G and N by removing only 3 edges.
    Let's consider removing any three edges from the set of edges directly connected to N:
    *   **Case 1: Remove (N, B), (N, C), (N, Y).** The edge (N, O) remains. A path from G to O still exists, for example: G-H-I-J-P-O. Therefore, the path G-H-I-J-P-O-N would still connect G and N.
    *   **Case 2: Remove (N, B), (N, C), (N, O).** The edge (N, Y) remains. A path from G to Y still exists, for example: G-H-I-J-P-O-Y (since O is connected to Y, and O is reachable from G). Therefore, the path G-H-I-J-P-O-Y-N would still connect G and N.
    *   **Case 3: Remove (N, B), (N, Y), (N, O).** The edge (N, C) remains. A path from G to C still exists, for example: G-H-I-C. Therefore, the path G-H-I-C-N would still connect G and N.
    *   **Case 4: Remove (N, C), (N, Y), (N, O).** The edge (N, B) remains. A path from G to B still exists, for example: G-H-B or G-F-E-B. Therefore, the path G-H-B-N (or G-F-E-B-N) would still connect G and N.

    In all cases where 3 edges directly connected to N are removed, at least one path to N remains active. This shows that removing 3 edges directly connected to N is not sufficient.

6.  **Consider other potential cut sets of size 3:**
    *   Let's consider the edges incident to G: (G,F), (G,X), (G,K), (G,H). If we remove any 3 of these, say (G,F), (G,X), (G,K), the edge (G,H) remains. A path like G-H-B-N would still connect G and N. Similarly for other combinations of 3 edges from G.
    *   Let's consider a cut set of 3 edges that are not directly incident to G or N. For example, consider the edges (H,B), (I,C), and (P,O).

7.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut of 4 edges is possible, and we have demonstrated that no cut of 3 edges is sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are I and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect I and E. I cannot remove I or E.

I will identify all distinct paths between I and E and look for node-disjoint paths. Node-disjoint paths are paths that do not share any intermediate nodes. The minimum number of nodes to remove will be equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem for nodes).

Let's list the immediate neighbors of I: H, C, P, J.
Let's list the immediate neighbors of E: P, L, M, B.

I can identify the following node-disjoint paths from I to E:

1.  **Path 1: I - P - E**
    *   This path uses the intermediate node P.

2.  **Path 2: I - C - B - E**
    *   This path uses the intermediate nodes C and B.
    *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 because it does not use P.

3.  **Path 3: I - J - M - E**
    *   This path uses the intermediate nodes J and M.
    *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (does not use P) and Path 2 (does not use C or B).

Let's verify if there are any other paths that are node-disjoint from these three.
*   **From I via H**: I - H - G. G is connected to F, K, X. F is connected to P (used in Path 1), G, X. K and X are only connected to G and F. None of these nodes (H, G, F, K, X) are connected to E or its neighbors (L, M, B) without going through P. So, the I-H branch does not lead to E without using P.
*   **From I via C**: Besides I - C - B - E (Path 2), C is connected to N, H.
    *   I - C - N - O - P - E: This path uses P, which is part of Path 1. So it's not node-disjoint from Path 1.
    *   I - C - N - Y - O - P - E: This path also uses P.
*   **From I via P**: Besides I - P - E (Path 1), P is connected to J, F, O.
    *   I - P - J - M - E: This path uses P (Path 1) and J, M (Path 3). It's not node-disjoint from Path 1 or Path 3.
    *   I - P - F - G - H - I (cycle): Does not lead to E.
*   **From I via J**: Besides I - J - M - E (Path 3), J is connected to P, D.
    *   I - J - P - E: This path uses P (Path 1) and J (Path 3). It's not node-disjoint from Path 1 or Path 3.
    *   I - J - D: D is not connected to E.

Since I have identified three node-disjoint paths (I-P-E, I-C-B-E, I-J-M-E), according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect I and E is at least 3.

To disconnect I and E, I must remove at least one node from each of these three paths:
*   To break Path 1 (I-P-E), I must remove node P.
*   To break Path 2 (I-C-B-E), I must remove either node C or node B.
*   To break Path 3 (I-J-M-E), I must remove either node J or node M.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 1 (for P) + 1 (for C or B) + 1 (for J or M) = 3 nodes.

An example of such a cut set is [P, C, J].
Let's verify that removing P, C, and J disconnects I and E:
*   If P is removed, Path 1 (I-P-E) is broken.
*   If C is removed, Path 2 (I-C-B-E) is broken.
*   If J is removed, Path 3 (I-J-M-E) is broken.

With P, C, and J removed, I's only remaining neighbor is H. H is connected to G. G is connected to F, K, X. None of these nodes (H, G, F, K, X) are connected to E or its remaining neighbors (L, M, B). Thus, no path remains between I and E.

Step-by-step reasoning. The two black nodes are labeled G and N. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node G to find the shortest path to node N.

1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(G, [G])]` (Node, Path)
    *   Visited: `{G}`

2.  **Level 0:**
    *   Dequeue `(G, [G])`.
    *   Neighbors of G: X, H, F.
    *   Enqueue `(X, [G, X])`, `(H, [G, H])`, `(F, [G, F])`.
    *   Visited: `{G, X, H, F}`.

3.  **Level 1:**
    *   Dequeue `(X, [G, X])`.
        *   Neighbors of X: K, F, G.
        *   K is not visited. Enqueue `(K, [G, X, K])`.
        *   F, G are visited.
    *   Dequeue `(H, [G, H])`.
        *   Neighbors of H: G, B, I.
        *   G is visited.
        *   B is not visited. Enqueue `(B, [G, H, B])`.
        *   I is not visited. Enqueue `(I, [G, H, I])`.
    *   Dequeue `(F, [G, F])`.
        *   Neighbors of F: X, K, G, E.
        *   X, K, G are visited.
        *   E is not visited. Enqueue `(E, [G, F, E])`.
    *   Visited: `{G, X, H, F, K, B, I, E}`.
    *   Current Queue: `[(K, [G, X, K]), (B, [G, H, B]), (I, [G, H, I]), (E, [G, F, E])]`

4.  **Level 2:**
    *   Dequeue `(K, [G, X, K])`.
        *   Neighbors of K: X, F, J.
        *   X, F are visited.
        *   J is not visited. Enqueue `(J, [G, X, K, J])`.
    *   Dequeue `(B, [G, H, B])`.
        *   Neighbors of B: E, H, C, N.
        *   E, H are visited.
        *   C is not visited. Enqueue `(C, [G, H, B, C])`.
        *   **N is the target node!** Path found: `[G, H, B, N]`.
        *   The length of this path is 3. Since this is a BFS, the first path found to the target node is guaranteed to be the shortest.

5.  **Verification:**
    *   Is G connected to H? Yes.
    *   Is H connected to B? Yes.
    *   Is B connected to N? Yes.
    *   The path `[G, H, B, N]` is valid and has a length of 3. No shorter path (length 1 or 2) was found during the BFS.

Image name: PGS_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and N. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node H is connected to: G, B, I.
    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, Y, O.

2.  **Consider a simple approach: isolating one of the target nodes.**
    *   To isolate H, we can remove all edges connected to it: (H, G), (H, B), (H, I). This would require removing 3 edges.
    *   To isolate N, we can remove all edges connected to it: (N, B), (N, C), (N, Y), (N, O). This would require removing 4 edges.
    *   Currently, 3 edges is the minimum candidate.

3.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges).**
    *   A single edge removal is unlikely to disconnect H and N, as there are clearly multiple paths between them. For example, removing (H, B) still leaves paths via G and I. Removing (N, B) still leaves paths via C, Y, and O.
    *   Let's try to find if 2 edges are sufficient. This would imply that there are at most 2 edge-disjoint paths between H and N. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least 3.

4.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths between H and N:**
    Let's try to find three paths from H to N that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: {(H, B), (B, N)}

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}

    *   **Path 3:** H - G - K - J - D - P - O - N
        Edges: {(H, G), (G, K), (K, J), (J, D), (D, P), (P, O), (O, N)}

5.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   Edges in Path 1: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (H, G), (G, K), (K, J), (J, D), (D, P), (P, O), (O, N)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these three paths are distinct. No edge is shared between any two of these paths. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths between H and N.

6.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:**
    Menger's Theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

7.  **Confirm the minimum cut set:**
    We already identified a cut set of size 3 by removing all edges directly connected to H: [(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]. Removing these three edges completely isolates H from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from N. Since we know the minimum is at least 3, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is indeed the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I']]}

Image name: Tu_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are G and I.
2.  **List all direct neighbors of G:** H, K, F.
3.  **List all direct neighbors of I:** H, P, J, C.
4.  **Systematically trace all paths from G to I:**
    *   **Path 1 (through H):** G - H - I
    *   **Paths through F:**
        *   G - F - P - I
        *   G - F - P - J - I
        *   G - F - E - P - I
        *   G - F - E - P - J - I
        *   G - F - E - B - C - I
    *   **Paths through K (these eventually merge with paths through F):**
        *   G - K - X - F - P - I
        *   G - K - X - F - P - J - I
        *   G - K - X - F - E - P - I
        *   G - K - X - F - E - P - J - I
        *   G - K - X - F - E - B - C - I

5.  **Analyze for single-node cuts:**
    *   If H is removed, paths like G-F-P-I still exist.
    *   If F is removed, path G-H-I still exists.
    *   If P is removed, path G-H-I and G-F-E-B-C-I still exist.
    *   If J is removed, path G-H-I and G-F-P-I still exist.
    *   If C is removed, path G-H-I and G-F-P-I still exist.
    *   No single node can disconnect G from I. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

6.  **Identify critical nodes for a two-node cut:**
    *   Observe that all paths from G to I can be broadly categorized into two groups based on their initial step from G:
        *   Group A: Paths starting with G - H. The only path in this group is G - H - I.
        *   Group B: Paths starting with G - F or G - K. All paths starting with G - K eventually pass through F (G - K - X - F). Therefore, all paths in this group (Group B) must pass through node F.
    *   To disconnect G from I, we must break all paths in Group A AND all paths in Group B.
    *   To break Group A, we must remove node H.
    *   To break Group B, we must remove node F (as all paths in this group pass through F).

7.  **Verify the proposed cut set [H, F]:**
    *   Removing H breaks the path G - H - I.
    *   Removing F breaks all paths that start G - F... and all paths that start G - K - X - F... (since X is connected to F).
    *   With H and F removed, G is only connected to K. K is connected to G and X. X is connected to K and F. Since F is removed, X is no longer connected to the rest of the graph towards I. Thus, G is completely disconnected from I.

8.  **Conclusion:** The set [H, F] successfully disconnects G from I, and it is of size 2, which is the minimum possible.

The final answer is \boxed{Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, F]}
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['E', 'H']}

        *   Path 1: E - B - H

        *   From E via F:
            *   Path 3: E - F - G - H
            *   Other paths like E - F - K - G - H, E - F - X - G - H, etc., all pass through F and G.
        *   From E via L:
            *   E - L - M - J - I - H (This path passes through I, so it's covered by Path 2's branch)
            *   E - L - M - J - P - I - H (This path also passes through I)
        *   From E via M:
            *   E - M - J - I - H (This path passes through I)
            *   E - M - J - P - I - H (This path also passes through I)

    *   **Paths through other neighbors of H:**
        *   From H via G:
            *   H - G - F - E (This is Path 3 in reverse)
            *   H - G - K - F - E (This is covered by Path 3's branch)
            *   H - G - X - F - E (This is covered by Path 3's branch)

    *   **Indirect paths involving C, N, O, P, J, D, K, X, Y:**
        *   E - B - C - I - H (This path passes through B and I)
        *   E - B - C - N - B - H (This path passes through B)
        *   E - I - C - B - H (This path passes through I and B)
        *   E - I - C - N - B - H (This path passes through I and B)
        *   All other longer paths (e.g., E - B - N - O - P - I - H) also pass through B and/or I.

3.  **Identify vertex-disjoint paths:** To find the minimum cut set, I look for vertex-disjoint paths (paths that share no intermediate nodes) between E and H.

    *   Path A: E - B - H (Intermediate node: B)
    *   Path B: E - I - H (Intermediate node: I)
    *   Path C: E - F - G - H (Intermediate nodes: F, G)

    These three paths are vertex-disjoint because their intermediate nodes (B, I, F, G) are all distinct.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Since there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths between E and H, Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect E and H is 3.

5.  **Determine the cut set:** To break all three identified vertex-disjoint paths, I must remove at least one node from each path (excluding E and H).
    *   To break Path A (E-B-H), I must remove node B.
    *   To break Path B (E-I-H), I must remove node I.
    *   To break Path C (E-F-G-H), I must remove either node F or node G.

    Therefore, a minimal cut set will consist of B, I, and either F or G.

6.  **Verify the cut set:** Let's choose the cut set [B, I, F].
    *   Removing B disconnects E from H via the E-B-H path and any other path that uses B as a bridge (e.g., E-B-C-I-H).
    *   Removing I disconnects E from H via the E-I-H path and any other path that uses I as a bridge (e.g., E-L-M-J-I-H, E-I-C-B-H).
    *   Removing F disconnects E from H via the E-F-G-H path and any other path that uses F as a bridge (e.g., E-F-K-G-H).
    *   After removing B, I, and F, there are no remaining paths from E to H. For example, E's remaining neighbors are L and M. Any path from L or M to H would have to go through J, then I or P. Since I is removed, and P is only connected to I, J, D, O (none of which lead to H without I or B), no path remains.

The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

    *   **Can we disconnect with 1 edge?**

    *   H - I - J - M - E - B

*   Removing (H, B) breaks the direct path.

To confirm that this is the *minimum* number of edges, I need to check if there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B. If there are, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum cut size is at least 3.

*   **Path 3:** H - I - C - B (Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)})

Upon inspection, the sets of edges for Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are completely disjoint. There are no shared edges between these three paths.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H and B must be at least 3. As I have also found a cut set of size 3, this is indeed the minimum.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify the direct connections of the target nodes:**
    *   Node G is connected to: K, X, F, E, H.
    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, O, Y.

2.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. I will try to find edge-disjoint paths from G to N.

3.  **Find edge-disjoint paths from G to N:**
    *   **Path 1:** Start from G, go through H, then B, then N.
        *   P1: G - H - B - N
        *   Edges: (G, H), (H, B), (B, N)

    *   **Path 2:** Start from G, go through E, then M, J, P, O, then N.
        *   P2: G - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   Edges: (G, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

    *   **Path 3:** Now I need a third path that does not use any of the edges from P1 or P2.
        *   Edges used so far: (G, H), (H, B), (B, N), (G, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N).
        *   From G, I can use (G, F).
        *   From F, I can use (F, E). (E) is available as a node, but (G,E) is used. (F,E) is not used.
        *   From E, I can use (E, H). (H) is available as a node, but (G,H) is used. (E,H) is not used.
        *   From H, I can use (H, C). (C) is available.
        *   From C, I can use (C, N). (N) is the target.
        *   P3: G - F - E - H - C - N
        *   Edges: (G, F), (F, E), (E, H), (H, C), (C, N)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness of P1, P2, and P3:**
    *   P1 edges: {(G, H), (H, B), (B, N)}
    *   P2 edges: {(G, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)}
    *   P3 edges: {(G, F), (F, E), (E, H), (H, C), (C, N)}
    *   Comparing the sets of edges, all edges are unique to their respective paths. For example, (G,H) is only in P1, (G,E) only in P2, (G,F) only in P3. (H,B) is only in P1, (E,M) only in P2, (F,E) only in P3, and so on.
    *   Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    *   Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

6.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges:**
    *   To achieve disconnection with 3 edges, I need to find a set of 3 edges that cuts all paths. A straightforward approach is to cut one edge from each of the identified edge-disjoint paths.
    *   From P1: (B, N)
    *   From P2: (O, N)
    *   From P3: (C, N)
    *   Proposed Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]

7.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   If edges (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N) are removed:
        *   Any path from G to N that passes through B is broken at (B, N).
        *   Any path from G to N that passes through C is broken at (C, N).
        *   Any path from G to N that passes through O is broken at (O, N).
        *   Node N is also connected to Y via (N, Y). However, Y is only connected to N and O. Since (O, N) is removed, O cannot reach N. Therefore, any path from G to N via Y (which would be G -> ... -> O -> Y -> N) is effectively broken because O cannot reach N.
    *   Thus, removing these three edges completely disconnects G from N.

Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3. I also found a cut set of exactly 3 edges that disconnects G and N. Therefore, the minimum is 3.

        -   Edges of Path 1: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`
        -   Edges of Path 2: `(E, F)`, `(F, G)`, `(G, H)`
        All edges in Path 3 are distinct from the edges in Path 1 and Path 2. For example, `(E, M)` is distinct from `(E, B)` and `(E, F)`. `(M, F)` is distinct from `(F, G)`. `(F, K)` is distinct from `(F, G)`. `(I, H)` is distinct from `(B, H)` and `(G, H)`.
        Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the lower bound:** Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal will disconnect E and H. A straightforward approach is to consider all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.
    Node H has three direct connections: `(B, H)`, `(G, H)`, and `(I, H)`.
    If these three edges are removed, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. If H is isolated, no path can exist between E and H.

6.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   **Cut Set:** `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`
    *   **Size:** 3 edges.
    *   **Effectiveness:** Removing these three edges severs all connections to node H. Therefore, no path can exist from E (or any other node) to H.
    *   **Minimality:** Since we established a lower bound of 3 edges, and we found a cut set of size 3, this cut set is minimal.

The final answer is based on the identification of 3 edge-disjoint paths and a corresponding cut set of 3 edges.

The final answer is structured as follows:

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F (black) and J (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between F and J.

First, I identify all direct connections from F to its neighbors and from J to its neighbors.
Neighbors of F: K, X, G, E
Neighbors of J: P, I, D, M

I will systematically explore all possible paths from F to J and identify critical edges that, if removed, would break these paths.

Let's categorize the initial branches from F:
1.  Paths starting F -> K
2.  Paths starting F -> X
3.  Paths starting F -> G
4.  Paths starting F -> E

Now, let's trace these branches towards J:

**Branch 1, 2, 3 (Paths through K, X, or G):**
*   Any path starting F -> K must go F -> K -> X -> G.
*   Any path starting F -> X must go F -> X -> G.
*   Any path starting F -> G goes directly to G.
So, all paths originating from F through K, X, or G converge at G. From G, the only outgoing edge towards the "J-side" of the graph is (G, H).
*   Path example: F -> G -> H -> I -> J
*   Path example: F -> G -> H -> I -> P -> J
*   Path example: F -> K -> X -> G -> H -> I -> J
*   Path example: F -> X -> G -> H -> I -> P -> J
To cut all these paths, the edge **(G, H)** must be removed.

**Branch 4 (Paths through E):**
From E, there are two main directions: to M or to B.

*   **Sub-branch 4a (Paths through M):**
    The only path from E to J via M is F -> E -> M -> J.
    To cut this path, the edge **(M, J)** must be removed. (Note: F-E-L-M-J also exists, but removing (M,J) or (E,M) would cut this too).

*   **Sub-branch 4b (Paths through B):**
    From E, a path can go F -> E -> B. From B, there are multiple ways to reach J:
    *   F -> E -> B -> H -> I -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> H -> I -> P -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> C -> I -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> C -> I -> P -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> C -> O -> P -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> C -> N -> O -> P -> J
    All these paths share the initial edge **(E, B)**. If this edge is removed, all paths from F that go through E and then B are disconnected from J.

**Proposed Cut Set:**
Based on the analysis above, the following three edges appear to be critical for disconnecting F from J:
1.  **(G, H)**: Breaks all paths from F that go through K, X, or G.
2.  **(M, J)**: Breaks all paths from F that go through E and then M.
3.  **(E, B)**: Breaks all paths from F that go through E and then B.

Let's verify this cut set: S = [(G, H), (M, J), (E, B)].
*   If a path starts F -> K, F -> X, or F -> G, it must use (G, H) to proceed towards J. Removing (G, H) breaks these.
*   If a path starts F -> E, it can go to M or B.
    *   If it goes to M, it must use (M, J) to reach J. Removing (M, J) breaks this.
    *   If it goes to B, it must use (E, B) to reach B. Removing (E, B) breaks this.
All possible paths from F to J are covered by the removal of these three edges.

**Minimality Check:**
Could we achieve disconnection with fewer than 3 edges?
*   **1 edge:** Clearly not possible, as there are multiple independent "entry points" to the J-side (e.g., F-E-M-J and F-G-H-I-J are distinct paths).
*   **2 edges:** Let's consider removing any two edges from our proposed cut set:
    *   If we don't remove (G, H), paths like F-G-H-I-J would remain.
    *   If we don't remove (M, J), paths like F-E-M-J would remain.
    *   If we don't remove (E, B), paths like F-E-B-C-I-J would remain.
    Since each of these edges is necessary to break a distinct "bundle" of paths, removing any two would leave at least one path intact. Therefore, 2 edges are not sufficient.

Another possible cut set of size 3 would be to remove all edges directly connected to J that are part of paths from F: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)]. This also works, as D is only connected to P and J, so if P is disconnected from J, D is irrelevant for paths from F.

Since we have found a cut set of 3 edges and determined that 2 edges are not sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (M, J), (E, B)]

Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from B to H:

After removing these three edges, there are no remaining paths from B to H. Since we established that at least 3 edges are required, and we found a set of 3 edges that achieves the disconnection, this is the minimum.

    *   To break all paths through M, we can remove the edge (M, J). This severs M's connection to J, effectively isolating J from any path coming via M or L.
    *   To break all paths through H, we can remove the edge (E, H). This severs E's connection to H, effectively isolating E from any path going via H, B, C, I, P, N, O, Y.

Image name: FD-K_task2_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and O. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H from O. I cannot remove H or O.

2.  **Explore paths from H to O:** I will systematically list distinct paths from H to O, focusing on the intermediate nodes.

    *   **Paths starting H -> B:**
        *   Path 1: H - B - C - O
        *   Path 2: H - B - N - O

    *   **Paths starting H -> I:**
        *   Path 3: H - I - C - O
        *   Path 4: H - I - P - O
        *   Path 5: H - I - J - P - O

    *   **Paths starting H -> E:**
        *   Path 6: H - E - M - J - P - O
        *   Path 7: H - E - L - M - J - P - O (This path also goes through M, J, P)

3.  **Identify vertex-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove, I can look for the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between H and O. Vertex-disjoint paths share no intermediate nodes.

    Let's try to find three such paths:
    *   **Path A:** H - B - N - O (Intermediate nodes: B, N)
    *   **Path B:** H - I - C - O (Intermediate nodes: I, C)
    *   **Path C:** H - E - M - J - P - O (Intermediate nodes: E, M, J, P)

    Let's check if these paths are vertex-disjoint (excluding H and O):
    *   Intermediate nodes for Path A: {B, N}
    *   Intermediate nodes for Path B: {I, C}
    *   Intermediate nodes for Path C: {E, M, J, P}

    These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually exclusive. Therefore, I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and O.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of vertices that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices (H and O in this case) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H and O.

    *   **Option 1: Removing the direct neighbors of H that lead to O.**
        The direct neighbors of H that lead to O are B, I, and E.
        If I remove nodes [B, I, E]:
        *   All paths starting with H-B... are broken.
        *   All paths starting with H-I... are broken.
        *   All paths starting with H-E... are broken.
        *   H is also connected to G and F, but G and F do not lead to O without passing through B, I, or E (e.g., H-G-F-E... would be broken by removing E).
        Therefore, removing [B, I, E] successfully disconnects H from O.

    *   **Option 2: Removing the direct neighbors of O that lead from H.**
        The direct neighbors of O that receive paths from H are C, N, and P. (Y is also a neighbor of O, but paths to Y must go through N, which is already covered).
        If I remove nodes [C, N, P]:
        *   All paths ending with C-O are broken.
        *   All paths ending with N-O are broken.
        *   All paths ending with P-O are broken.
        Therefore, removing [C, N, P] successfully disconnects H from O.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, and I have identified a cut set of size 3 (e.g., [B, I, E] or [C, N, P]), the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, E]

    *   Therefore, all paths from F to J must pass through either node E or node G.

    *   If I remove node E, F can still reach J via paths starting with F -> G (e.g., F -> G -> H -> I -> J).
    *   If I remove node G, F can still reach J via paths starting with F -> E (e.g., F -> E -> M -> J).

    *   Therefore, removing both E and G successfully disconnects F from J.
5.  **Confirm Minimum Size:** I have found a cut set of size 2 ({E, G}). To confirm this is the minimum, I can look for vertex-disjoint paths between F and J.
    *   Path 1: F - E - M - J (Intermediate nodes: E, M)
    *   Path 2: F - G - H - I - J (Intermediate nodes: G, H, I)
    These two paths are vertex-disjoint (they do not share any intermediate nodes). According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect F and J is at least the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths, which is 2.
6.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut set of size 2 has been found, and it's confirmed that at least 2 nodes are required, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

2.  **Identify all direct connections from L:** L is connected to E and M.
3.  **Identify all direct connections to J:** J is connected to P, D, I, and M.

4.  **Analyze paths from L to J:**
    Any path from L to J must start with either L-E or L-M.
    Any path from L to J must end with J-P, J-D, J-I, or J-M (in reverse).

    Let's list some representative paths:
    *   L - M - J (Direct path)
    *   L - E - M - J
    *   L - E - F - K - J
    *   L - E - F - G - X - K - J
    *   L - E - F - G - H - I - J
    *   L - E - B - H - I - J
    *   L - E - B - C - I - J
    *   L - E - B - C - O - P - J
    *   L - E - B - C - N - O - P - J

5.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
    *   **If M is removed:**
        The path L - M - J is broken.
        The path L - E - M - J is broken.
        However, many paths starting with L - E - ... still exist, for example, L - E - F - K - J, L - E - B - H - I - J, etc.
        Therefore, removing M alone is not sufficient.
    *   **If E is removed:**
        All paths starting with L - E - ... are broken (e.g., L - E - M - J, L - E - F - K - J, L - E - B - H - I - J).
        However, the path L - M - J still exists.
        Therefore, removing E alone is not sufficient.
    *   **If any other single node is removed (e.g., F, B, H, I, P, K, G, X, C, O, N, D, Y):**
        Removing any single node other than E or M would only break a subset of the paths, leaving at least one path through E and at least one path through M (or a path that uses both E and M, like L-E-M-J, which would be broken by removing E or M, but not by removing a node further down the path if L-M-J still exists). For example, removing F would break paths like L-E-F-K-J, but L-M-J and L-E-B-H-I-J would remain. Removing B would break paths like L-E-B-H-I-J, but L-M-J and L-E-F-K-J would remain. Removing D or Y would not disconnect L from J as they are leaf nodes not on any path between L and J.
    *   Since removing any single node does not disconnect L from J, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

6.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Consider the nodes directly connected to L: E and M.
    *   If both E and M are removed, L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    *   Since L is isolated, no path can exist between L and J.
    *   This set of nodes, [E, M], successfully disconnects L from J.
    *   The size of this cut set is 2.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut set of size 1 is not possible, and we have found a cut set of size 2, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, B and M.

2.  **Analyze connections to M:** Node M is directly connected to three other nodes: E, L, and J. This means any path from B to M must use one of these three edges: (E, M), (L, M), or (J, M).

3.  **Consider a cut set by removing all direct edges to M:**
    If we remove the edges (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M), node M becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between B and M.
    This set of edges, {(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)}, is a valid cut set, and its size is 3.

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut set exists (i.e., 1 or 2 edges):**
    *   **Can 1 edge disconnect B and M?** No, because there are multiple independent routes to M. For example, removing (E, M) would still leave paths through L (e.g., B-H-E-L-M) and J (e.g., B-C-I-J-M).
    *   **Can 2 edges disconnect B and M?** Let's test this systematically. Since all paths to M must use one of the edges (E, M), (L, M), or (J, M), we can analyze what happens if we remove any two of these three critical edges:
        *   **Scenario 1: Remove (E, M) and (L, M).**
            After removing these two edges, node M is still connected to J via the edge (J, M). Paths from B to M through J still exist, for example:
            *   B - H - I - J - M
            *   B - C - I - J - M
            *   B - N - O - P - J - M
            Since paths still exist, removing (E, M) and (L, M) is not sufficient.

        *   **Scenario 2: Remove (E, M) and (J, M).**
            After removing these two edges, node M is still connected to L via the edge (L, M). Paths from B to M through L still exist, for example:
            *   B - H - E - L - M (Note: E is still connected to L, and L to M)
            *   B - H - G - E - L - M
            Since paths still exist, removing (E, M) and (J, M) is not sufficient.

        *   **Scenario 3: Remove (L, M) and (J, M).**
            After removing these two edges, node M is still connected to E via the edge (E, M). Paths from B to M through E still exist, for example:
            *   B - H - E - M
            *   B - H - G - E - M
            *   B - H - G - F - E - M
            Since paths still exist, removing (L, M) and (J, M) is not sufficient.

    *   **What if the two removed edges are not directly connected to M?**
        If we remove any two edges that are *not* (E, M), (L, M), or (J, M), then at least two (or all three) of these direct connections to M will remain. For example, if (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M) all remain, then there are still multiple paths to M (e.g., B-H-E-M, B-H-E-L-M, B-C-I-J-M). It would be impossible to cut all these diverse paths with just two edges further upstream in the graph. The analysis above (Scenarios 1-3) demonstrates that even if we cut two of the direct edges to M, a path still remains through the third direct edge. This implies that any two edges, regardless of their position, cannot disconnect B and M.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since removing any two edges is insufficient to disconnect B and M, and removing three specific edges (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M) *does* disconnect them, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

The final answer is based on the fact that M has three distinct "entry points" (E, L, J), and paths exist to each of these points from B. To disconnect M, all these entry points must be severed.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]

        Edges: {(G,H), (H,I)}

    *   **Removing (P, J):** This breaks all paths that go through P to reach J. Examples: E-I-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-C-N-O-P-J.

    After removing these three edges, J is only connected to D. Node D is connected to J and P. Since (P, J) is removed, D cannot serve as a bridge to E. Therefore, J is completely isolated from E. This cut set has 3 edges.

5.  **Determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., 2 edges) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that removing any two edges is not sufficient to disconnect E and J.
    Let's consider the three critical edges identified: (M, J), (I, J), (P, J).
    *   **Case A: Remove (M, J) and (I, J).**
        *   Paths through M-J are broken.
        *   Paths through I-J are broken.
        *   However, paths through P-J are still intact. For example, E can reach P via E-I-P (if (E,I) is not cut) or E-B-C-O-P (if (E,B) is not cut). Since (P, J) is not removed, paths like E-I-P-J or E-B-C-O-P-J still exist. Thus, E and J are still connected.
    *   **Case B: Remove (M, J) and (P, J).**
        *   Paths through M-J are broken.
        *   Paths through P-J are broken.
        *   However, paths through I-J are still intact. For example, E can reach I via E-I (if (E,I) is not cut) or E-B-I (if (E,B) is not cut). Since (I, J) is not removed, paths like E-I-J or E-B-I-J still exist. Thus, E and J are still connected.
    *   **Case C: Remove (I, J) and (P, J).**
        *   Paths through I-J are broken.
        *   Paths through P-J are broken.
        *   However, paths through M-J are still intact. For example, E can reach M via E-M (if (E,M) is not cut) or E-L-M (if (E,L) is not cut). Since (M, J) is not removed, paths like E-M-J or E-L-M-J still exist. Thus, E and J are still connected.

    Since removing any two of these three critical edges leaves at least one path open, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

2.  **Identify direct connections to N:** Node N is directly connected to B, C, and Y.

3.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   If I remove all edges connected to H: (H, G), (H, I), (H, B), then H would be completely isolated. This would require removing 3 edges.
    *   If I remove all edges connected to N: (N, B), (N, C), (N, Y), then N would be completely isolated. This would also require removing 3 edges.
    These are potential cut sets of size 3.

4.  **Check for smaller cut sets (e.g., 1 or 2 edges):**
    *   **Can 1 edge disconnect H and N?** No, because there are clearly multiple distinct paths between H and N. For example, H-B-N is one path, and H-I-C-N is another. Removing one edge would not break all paths.
    *   **Can 2 edges disconnect H and N?** To determine this, I will try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and N. If I can find 3 or more edge-disjoint paths, then 2 edges will not be sufficient.

5.  **Find edge-disjoint paths between H and N:**
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges used: (H, B), (B, N)

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges used: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses entirely different edges.

    *   **Path 3:** I need a third path that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        Starting from H, the only remaining unused direct connection is (H, G).
        From G, I need to find a path to N without using (H, B), (B, N), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N).

        *   Let's try to reach N via C or Y, without using (C, N).

        All edges in Path 3 are distinct from the edges in Path 1 and Path 2.

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are H (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and N.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of the target nodes:
*   Neighbors of H: E, G, B, I
*   Neighbors of N: B, C, O, Y

Next, I will identify distinct paths from H to N. To find the minimum number of edges to remove, I can look for edge-disjoint paths between H and N. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (edge-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

Let's find edge-disjoint paths from H to N:

1.  **Path 1: H - B - N**
    *   Edges: (H, B), (B, N)

2.  **Path 2: H - I - C - N**
    *   Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)

3.  **Path 3: H - E - M - J - P - O - N**
    *   Edges: (H, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
*   The initial edges from H are (H, B), (H, I), and (H, E), which are distinct.
*   The final edges into N are (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N), which are also distinct.
*   All intermediate edges in Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are also distinct from each other. For example, (I, C) is not used in Path 1 or Path 3, and (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O) are not used in Path 1 or Path 2.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection. A common strategy is to cut all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes, excluding those that lead to parts of the graph not connected to the other target node.

Consider the edges directly connected to N that can be reached from H: (B, N), (C, N), (O, N). The edge (Y, N) does not need to be cut because Y is not reachable from H (Y is only connected to N and O, and O is connected to N, C, P, J, M, E, H, I, B, G, F, K, X, L, D).

Let's propose the cut set: `[(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]`.

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects H from N:
*   Removing (B, N) breaks Path 1 (H-B-N) and any other path that might use (B, N) (e.g., H-I-C-B-N, H-E-M-J-P-O-C-B-N).
*   Removing (C, N) breaks Path 2 (H-I-C-N) and any other path that might use (C, N) (e.g., H-B-C-N).
*   Removing (O, N) breaks Path 3 (H-E-M-J-P-O-N) and any other path that might use (O, N) (e.g., H-I-P-O-N, H-B-C-O-N).

After removing these three edges, N is only connected to Y. Since Y is not connected to H or any node reachable from H, N is completely disconnected from H.

Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of 3 edges, this is indeed the minimum number of edges.

An alternative minimal cut set could be `[(H, B), (H, I), (H, E)]`, which cuts the paths at their origin from H. This also has a size of 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]

Therefore, both edges (J, D) and (P, D) must be removed to ensure no path exists between F and D.
7.  **Verify the cut set:** Removing [(J, D), (P, D)] completely isolates D from the rest of the graph, thus ensuring no path exists from F to D. This set contains 2 edges.
8.  **Confirm minimality:** As established in step 5, removing only one edge is not enough. Therefore, 2 is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

1.  **Path through M:**
    *   L - M - J
    This path requires node M.

2.  **Paths through E and then P:**
    *   L - E - P - J
    *   L - E - P - I - J
    *   L - E - B - O - P - J
    *   L - E - B - N - O - P - J
    *   L - E - B - C - N - O - P - J
    All these paths require node P.

    *   L - E - B - C - I - J
    *   L - E - B - C - H - I - J
    *   L - E - B - N - C - I - J
    *   L - E - B - O - N - C - I - J
    All these paths require node I.

Let's test if removing two nodes is sufficient:
*   **If I remove M and P:**
    *   The path L-M-J is broken.
    *   All paths from L-E that go through P (e.g., L-E-P-J, L-E-B-O-P-J) are broken.
    *   However, paths that go through I (without passing P) would still exist, for example: L - E - B - C - I - J. So, [M, P] is not enough.

*   **If I remove M and I:**
    *   The path L-M-J is broken.
    *   All paths from L-E that go through I (e.g., L-E-B-C-I-J, L-E-P-I-J) are broken.
    *   However, paths that go through P (without passing I) would still exist, for example: L - E - P - J. So, [M, I] is not enough.

*   **If I remove P and I:**
    *   All paths from L-E that go through P (e.g., L-E-P-J, L-E-B-O-P-J) are broken.
    *   All paths from L-E that go through I (e.g., L-E-B-C-I-J, L-E-P-I-J) are broken.
    *   However, the path L - M - J would still exist. So, [P, I] is not enough.

Since removing any two nodes from the set {M, P, I} is not sufficient to disconnect L and J, I must consider removing all three.

**If I remove M, P, and I:**
*   The path L-M-J is broken (by removing M).
*   All paths from L-E that go through P are broken (by removing P).
*   All paths from L-E that go through I are broken (by removing I).
After removing M, P, and I, there are no remaining nodes directly connected to J that are also reachable from L. Therefore, L and J are completely disconnected.

The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

        *   B -> H -> G -> K -> F -> P -> E -> M
        *   B -> H -> G -> X -> F -> P -> E -> M

        *   This forms another cut set: [E, P].

    *   Neighbors of J: D, P, M, I
    *   Neighbors of F: E, K, X, G, H

    *   Removing all edges connected to F: (E,F), (K,F), (X,F), (G,F), (H,F). This is 5 edges.
    So, a cut of 4 edges is possible. We need to check if a smaller cut (2 or 3 edges) exists.

4.  **Analyze the graph structure and potential bottlenecks:**
    The graph can be conceptually divided into a "J-side" and an "F-side".
    *   **J-side nodes:** J, D, P, M, I, O, Y, N, C, L, B
    *   **F-side nodes:** F, E, K, X, G, H
    Any path from J to F must cross the boundary between these two sets of nodes. Let's list all edges that connect a node from the J-side to a node from the F-side:
    *   (L,E): L (J-side) to E (F-side)
    *   (B,E): B (J-side) to E (F-side)
    *   (I,H): I (J-side) to H (F-side)
    *   (C,H): C (J-side) to H (F-side)
    These are the only four edges that connect the J-side to the F-side. If we remove all of them, J and F will be disconnected. This gives a cut set of size 4: `[(L,E), (B,E), (I,H), (C,H)]`.

5.  **Check for a smaller cut (size 2 or 3):**
    *   **Can we disconnect with 2 edges?**
        Let's test some critical pairs of edges:
        *   Removing (E,F) and (H,F):
            Path J-I-H-G-F still exists. (Not a cut)
        *   Removing (L,E) and (I,H):
            Path J-M-O-N-B-E-F still exists. Path J-M-O-C-H-F still exists. (Not a cut)
        *   Removing (B,E) and (C,H):
            Path J-M-L-E-F still exists. Path J-I-H-F still exists. (Not a cut)
        It appears that any pair of edges leaves at least one path between J and F. This suggests that the minimum cut size is greater than 2.

    *   **Can we disconnect with 3 edges?**
        Let's try combinations of 3 edges, focusing on cutting the main "channels" or "entry points" to the F-side (E, H, G, X, K).
        The main "channels" from the J-side to the F-side are through nodes E and H.
        *   **Channel 1 (through E):** Paths reach E via L or B.
            Edges: (L,E), (B,E).
        *   **Channel 2 (through H):** Paths reach H via I or C.
            Edges: (I,H), (C,H).

        Let's try to cut these channels with 3 edges:
        *   **Attempt 1: `[(L,E), (B,E), (I,H)]`**
            - (L,E) and (B,E) cut all paths going through E.
            - (I,H) cuts all paths from J through I to H (e.g., J-I-H-F, J-I-H-G-F, etc.).
            - **Remaining paths:** Paths from J through C to H (e.g., J-M-O-C-H-F, J-P-O-C-H-G-F, etc.) are not cut. So, this set is insufficient.

        *   **Attempt 2: `[(L,E), (B,E), (C,H)]`**
            - (L,E) and (B,E) cut all paths going through E.
            - (C,H) cuts all paths from J through C to H (e.g., J-M-O-C-H-F, J-P-O-C-H-G-F, etc.).
            - **Remaining paths:** Paths from J through I to H (e.g., J-I-H-F, J-I-H-G-F, etc.) are not cut. So, this set is insufficient.

        *   **Attempt 3: `[(L,E), (I,H), (C,H)]`**
            - (L,E) cuts the path J-M-L-E-F.
            - (I,H) and (C,H) cut all paths going through H.
            - **Remaining paths:** Paths from J through B to E (e.g., J-M-O-N-B-E-F, J-P-O-C-B-E-F, etc.) are not cut. So, this set is insufficient.

            - (I,H) and (C,H) cut all paths going through H.
            - **Remaining paths:** Paths from J through L to E (e.g., J-M-L-E-F) are not cut. So, this set is insufficient.

            - (H,F) cuts paths ending in H-F.
            - (G,F) cuts paths ending in G-F.
            - **Remaining paths:** Paths like J-I-H-X-F or J-I-H-G-K-F are not cut. So, this set is insufficient.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** H and N.

2.  **Systematically find edge-disjoint paths from H to N:**

        Comparing edges of Path 1 and Path 2:
        Path 1: `(H, B), (B, N)`
        Path 2: `(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)`
        There are no common edges. So, Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** Next, let's find a path from H to N that does not use any edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        We need to find a path that avoids `(H, B), (B, N), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)`.

        Path 2: `(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)`
        Path 3: `(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, N)`
        There are no common edges. So, Path 2 and Path 3 are edge-disjoint.

3.  **Conclusion on edge-disjoint paths:** We have successfully identified 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N. This means, by Menger's Theorem, that the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H and N is at least 3.

4.  **Identify a minimal cut set:** To confirm that 3 edges are sufficient, we need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects H and N. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.

3.  **Find Vertex-Disjoint Paths:**

        *   This is a direct path between G and I through node H.

    *   **Path 2 (P2): G - K - J - I**

        *   Nodes involved (excluding G and I): {K, J}
