        *   **Remove I and P:** Paths like H -> B -> C -> O and H -> G -> F -> E -> B -> C -> O still exist.

        *   **Remove B and P:** Paths like H -> I -> O still exist.
        *   **Remove C and N:** Paths like H -> I -> O and H -> I -> P -> O still exist, as do paths through B and G that lead to P (e.g., H -> B -> E -> L -> M -> J -> P -> O).
        *   **Remove J and I:** Paths like H -> B -> C -> O and H -> G -> F -> E -> B -> C -> O still exist.
    *   Based on these checks, it appears that no combination of 2 nodes can completely disconnect H from O. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

6.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 3:**
    *   Consider removing all direct neighbors of H: {G, I, B}.
    *   If nodes G, I, and B are removed, H becomes an isolated node (except for its connection to O, which is not allowed to be removed).
    *   Since H has no remaining connections to any other non-black node, no path can exist from H to O.
    *   This set of nodes [G, I, B] successfully disconnects H from O.

7.  **Conclusion:** We have shown that a cut set of size 1 or 2 is not sufficient, and we have found a cut set of size 3. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

    *   If I remove E: Paths through F and J remain.

    *   If I remove J: All paths through L, E, and F remain.
    *   No single node can disconnect M and B.

4.  **Analyze for a two-node cut:**
    The paths can be broadly divided into two main "branches" from M:
    *   **Left Branch:** Paths going through L, E, or F. These paths eventually reach B via E or G.
    *   **Right Branch:** Paths going through J. These paths eventually reach B via P, I, O, C, or N.

First, I identify all nodes directly connected to E. These are B, F, L, and M.
Any path from H to E must pass through one of these nodes.
Node L is only connected to E and has no other connections that lead back to H. Therefore, L cannot be an intermediate node on a path from H to E, and removing it would not disconnect H from E.
So, any path from H to E must pass through B, F, or M. This means that the set {B, F, M} is a potential cut set. Its size is 3.

Now, I need to check if a smaller cut set (size 1 or 2) exists.

**Checking for a cut set of size 1:**
*   If I remove B: Paths through F and M would still exist (e.g., H-G-F-E, H-I-J-M-E). So, B alone is not enough.
*   If I remove F: Paths through B and M would still exist (e.g., H-C-N-B-E, H-I-J-M-E). So, F alone is not enough.
*   If I remove M: Paths through B and F would still exist (e.g., H-C-N-B-E, H-G-F-E). So, M alone is not enough.
Therefore, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.

**Checking for a cut set of size 2:**
I need to remove two nodes from the set {B, F, M} and see if any path remains.
*   If I remove B and F: Paths through M would still exist (e.g., H-I-J-M-E). So, {B, F} is not enough.
*   If I remove B and M: Paths through F would still exist (e.g., H-G-F-E). So, {B, M} is not enough.
*   If I remove F and M: Paths through B would still exist (e.g., H-C-N-B-E). So, {F, M} is not enough.
Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

Since a cut set of size 1 or 2 is not possible, the minimum cut set must be of size 3.
The set {B, F, M} successfully disconnects H from E because all paths from H to E must pass through at least one of these three nodes. Once B, F, and M are removed, E is only connected to L, which is not connected to H.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, F, M]

*   Removing I will cut all paths in Group 2.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut is possible:** To prove that 3 is the minimum number of edges, I need to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If there are `k` edge-disjoint paths, then at least `k` edges must be removed to disconnect the nodes.

    *   Path 3 edges: `{(E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)}`

3.  **Check for edge-disjointness of these paths:**
    *   Edges in Path 1: {(E, B), (B, H)}
    *   Edges in Path 2: {(E, I), (I, H)}
    *   Edges in Path 3: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}

    Upon inspection, these three paths do not share any common edges. For example, (E,B) is only in Path 1, (E,I) is only in Path 2, and (E,F) is only in Path 3. Similarly for the edges connected to H: (B,H) is only in Path 1, (I,H) is only in Path 2, and (G,H) is only in Path 3. The intermediate edges (F,G) are also unique to Path 3.

    Since we have found three edge-disjoint paths between E and H, by Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E and H.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    To disconnect H from E, we need to remove all connections from H to the rest of the graph. The edges directly connected to H are (H, G), (H, B), and (H, I).
    If we remove these three edges:
    *   (H, G) is removed, breaking Path 3 (E-F-G-H).
    *   (H, B) is removed, breaking Path 1 (E-B-H).
    *   (H, I) is removed, breaking Path 2 (E-I-H).

    After removing these three edges, node H becomes isolated from all other nodes, including E. Therefore, no path can exist between E and H.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We have identified three edge-disjoint paths, meaning a minimum of 3 edges must be removed. We have also found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects E and H. Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

First, I will try to find edge-disjoint paths between G and I. If I can find N edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is at least N.

Let's identify the neighbors of I: H, P, J, C.

    *   If we remove the edges (F, E), (F, X), and (F, K), node F becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. This means no path can exist from F to J.
    *   This constitutes a cut set of size 3: `[(F, E), (F, X), (F, K)]`.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges required is at most 3.

2.  **Identify all edges connected to J:**
    *   J is connected to M, P, D, and I.
    *   If we remove the edges (J, M), (J, P), (J, D), and (J, I), node J becomes isolated.
    *   This constitutes a cut set of size 4. Since we already found a cut of size 3, this set is not minimal.

3.  **Determine if a cut of size 2 is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we must show that no set of 2 edges can disconnect F from J. Let's consider various scenarios for removing any two edges:

    *   **Scenario A: Removing two edges directly connected to F.**
        *   If we remove (F, E) and (F, X), a path from F to J still exists via K (e.g., F-K-G-H-I-J).
        *   If we remove (F, E) and (F, K), a path from F to J still exists via X (e.g., F-X-G-H-I-J).
        *   If we remove (F, X) and (F, K), a path from F to J still exists via E (e.g., F-E-M-J).
        *   Thus, removing any two edges directly connected to F is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario B: Removing two edges directly connected to J.**
        *   J has 4 direct connections: (J, M), (J, P), (J, D), (J, I). Removing any two of these edges will leave at least two other connections, allowing paths to J to still exist. For example, if (J, M) and (J, P) are removed, paths via D (e.g., F-X-G-H-B-N-Y-O-P-J is cut, but F-X-G-H-I-J is still open) and I (e.g., F-E-B-C-I-J) would still be possible.
        *   Thus, removing any two edges directly connected to J is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario C: Removing two "internal" edges (not directly connected to F or J).**
        Let's identify the "bottleneck" edges that connect the "F-side" of the graph (nodes reachable from F without passing through J) to the "J-side" (nodes from which J is reachable without passing through F).
        *   F-side nodes: {F, E, X, K, G, H, L, B}
        *   J-side nodes: {J, M, P, D, I, C, O, Y, N}
        *   The edges connecting these two sets are:
            1.  (E, M)
            2.  (L, M)
            3.  (B, C)
            4.  (B, N)
            5.  (H, I)

        Let's consider the "channels" or distinct routes these edges represent:
        *   **Channel 1 (via M):** Paths like F-E-M-J and F-E-L-M-J. To cut all paths through M, we need to remove at least one of (E, M) or (L, M).
        *   **Channel 2 (via B-C):** Paths like F-...-B-C-I-J or F-...-B-C-O-P-J. This channel is cut by (B, C).
        *   **Channel 3 (via B-N):** Paths like F-...-B-N-C-I-J or F-...-B-N-Y-O-P-J. This channel is cut by (B, N).
        *   **Channel 4 (via H-I):** Paths like F-...-H-I-J. This channel is cut by (H, I).

        To disconnect F from J, we need to cut at least one edge from each of these four conceptual channels.
        Let's test if removing any two edges from the set {(E, M), (L, M), (B, C), (B, N), (H, I)} is sufficient:
        *   If we remove (E, M) and (B, C):
            *   Path F-E-L-M-J (Channel 1, via L) is still open.
            *   Path F-X-G-H-I-J (Channel 4) is still open.
            *   Path F-E-B-N-C-I-J (Channel 3) is still open.
            *   Thus, not sufficient.
        *   Similar analysis for any other pair of edges from this set would show that at least two of these four channels remain open.

    *   **Scenario D: Removing one edge connected to F/J and one internal edge.**
        *   If we remove (F, E) and (B, C):

            *   Paths starting with F-X or F-K and going through B-N are still open. For example, F-X-G-H-B-N-C-I-J (Channel 3) is still open.
            *   Thus, not sufficient.

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt2_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I. I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect them.

First, I will identify edge-disjoint paths between G and I.

1.  **Path 1 (P1): G - E - I**
    Edges used: `(G, E)`, `(E, I)`

2.  **Path 2 (P2): G - H - I**
    Edges used: `(G, H)`, `(H, I)`
    P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint as they do not share any common edges.

3.  Now, I need to find a third path (P3) that is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2. This means P3 cannot use any of the edges `(G, E)`, `(E, I)`, `(G, H)`, or `(H, I)`.
    Let's start from G and try a different initial edge, for example, `(G, F)`.
    **G - F**
    From F, we can go to E.
    **G - F - E**
    From E, we cannot go directly to I (as `(E, I)` is used by P1). We also cannot use `(G, E)` to reach E from G.
    From E, we can go to H.
    **G - F - E - H**
    From H, we cannot go directly to I (as `(H, I)` is used by P2). We also cannot use `(G, H)` to reach H from G.
    From H, we can go to B.
    **G - F - E - H - B**
    From B, we can go to C or N. Let's choose C.
    **G - F - E - H - B - C**
    From C, we can go to I.
    **G - F - E - H - B - C - I**
    Edges used for P3: `(G, F)`, `(F, E)`, `(E, H)`, `(H, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`.
    Let's verify if P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2:
    P1 edges: `{(G, E), (E, I)}`
    P2 edges: `{(G, H), (H, I)}`
    P3 edges: `{(G, F), (F, E), (E, H), (H, B), (B, C), (C, I)}`
    All edges in P3 are distinct from the edges in P1 and P2. Therefore, P1, P2, and P3 are three edge-disjoint paths.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect G from I must be at least 3.

Next, I will identify a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects G from I.
Consider the edges directly connected to G that lead towards the rest of the graph: `(G, E)`, `(G, H)`, and `(G, F)`.
Let's propose the cut set: `[(G, E), (G, H), (G, F)]`.

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects G from I:
1.  **Remove (G, E):** This cuts P1 (G-E-I) and any other path starting G-E-...
2.  **Remove (G, H):** This cuts P2 (G-H-I) and any other path starting G-H-...
3.  **Remove (G, F):** This cuts P3 (G-F-E-H-B-C-I) and any other path starting G-F-...

After removing these three edges, G is only connected to K and X.
-   K is connected to G, X, F.
-   X is connected to G, K, F.
-   F is connected to G, K, X, E.

Since `(G, F)` is removed, G cannot reach F.
Since `(G, E)` is removed, G cannot reach E.
Since `(G, H)` is removed, G cannot reach H.

All paths from G to I must pass through E, H, or F (as these are the only nodes connected to G that can lead to I's side of the graph). Since G can no longer reach E, H, or F, G is completely disconnected from I.

This cut set of 3 edges successfully disconnects G from I. Since we established that at least 3 edges are required, this cut set is minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, E), (G, H), (G, F)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are G and I.
2.  **Goal:** Find the minimum number of non-target nodes to remove to disconnect G and I.
3.  **Systematic exploration of paths:** I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between G and I. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

    *   **Path 1:** I observe a direct path from G to I through node H.
        `G - H - I`
        The intermediate node in this path is H.

    *   **Path 2:** I look for a path from G to I that does not use H.
        From G, I can go to X. From X, I can go to K. From K, I can go to J. From J, I can go to P. From P, I can go to I.
        `G - X - K - J - P - I`
        The intermediate nodes in this path are X, K, J, P. This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (except for G and I).

    *   **Path 3:** I look for a path from G to I that does not use H, X, K, J, or P.
        From G, I can go to F. From F, I can go to E. From E, I can go to B. From B, I can go to C. From C, I can go to I.
        `G - F - E - B - C - I`
        The intermediate nodes in this path are F, E, B, C. This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (except for G and I).

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between G and I:
    *   P1: {G, H, I}
    *   P2: {G, X, K, J, P, I}
    *   P3: {G, F, E, B, C, I}

    Since there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect G and I.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:** To disconnect G and I, I need to remove at least one node from each of these three vertex-disjoint paths.
    *   From P1: Remove H.
    *   From P2: Remove J (or X, K, P).
    *   From P3: Remove E (or F, B, C).

    A possible cut set is [H, J, E]. Let's verify this:
    *   Removing H breaks `G - H - I`.
    *   Removing J breaks `G - X - K - J - P - I`.
    *   Removing E breaks `G - F - E - B - C - I`.

    With H, J, and E removed, all three identified vertex-disjoint paths are broken. I have also checked other potential paths and confirmed that they would also be broken by this set (e.g., any path going through J or E would be broken). For example, paths like `G - X - K - J - M - L - E - B - C - I` would be broken by J or E.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [H, J, E]

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between M and B.

First, I identify all direct connections from M and to B:
*   Node M is connected to: E, L, K.
*   Node B is connected to: E, H, C, N.

Any path from M to B must start from M and end at B, traversing a sequence of connected nodes. This means any path must pass through one of M's direct neighbors (E, L, K) and eventually reach one of B's direct neighbors (E, H, C, N) before reaching B.

Let's analyze the possible "entry points" from the M-side of the graph to the B-side of the graph.
All paths from M to B can be categorized based on which neighbor of B they ultimately use:

**Category 1: Paths that reach B via node E.**
*   M-E-B (direct path)
*   M-L-E-B
*   M-K-F-E-B
*   M-K-X-F-E-B
*   M-K-X-G-F-E-B
All these paths share the common edge (E, B) as their final segment to reach B. Therefore, removing the edge (E, B) will disconnect all paths in this category.

**Category 2: Paths that reach B via nodes H, C, or N.**
To reach H, C, or N from M, M must first traverse through its neighbors K or L.
*   If M goes through L, it can only reach E (M-L-E). Since we are considering paths *not* using E to reach B (as those are covered by Category 1), this branch from L does not lead to H, C, or N without passing through E.
*   If M goes through K, it can reach X or F.
    *   From X, M can reach G or F.
    *   From F, M can reach G or E.
    *   From G, M can reach X, F, or H.
    It becomes clear that any path from M to H, C, or N must eventually pass through node G. From node G, the only edge that leads towards H (and subsequently I, C, P, O, N, D, J, Y, and B) is the edge (G, H).
    Let's list some example paths:
    *   M-K-X-G-H-B
    *   M-K-F-G-H-B
    *   M-K-X-F-G-H-B
    *   M-K-X-G-H-I-C-B
    *   M-K-X-G-H-I-P-O-C-B
    *   M-K-X-G-H-I-C-N-B
    *   M-K-X-G-H-I-P-O-N-B
    All these paths, and any other path from M to B that does not use the (E,B) edge, must pass through the edge (G, H) to cross from the "left" part of the graph (containing M, K, L, X, F, G) to the "right" part (containing H, I, C, N, O, P, D, J, Y, B). Therefore, removing the edge (G, H) will disconnect all paths in this category.

**Conclusion:**
To completely disconnect M from B, we must break all paths in Category 1 and all paths in Category 2.
*   Removing (E, B) breaks all paths that use E to reach B.
*   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that use G to reach H (and then to B via H, C, or N).

These two edges, (E, B) and (G, H), are sufficient to disconnect M and B.
To confirm minimality, consider if removing only one edge would suffice:
*   If only (E, B) is removed, paths like M-K-X-G-H-B would still exist.
*   If only (G, H) is removed, paths like M-E-B would still exist.
Therefore, both edges are necessary, and the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, B), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['M', 'E'], ['M', 'J'], ['M', 'L']], [['M', 'J'], ['F', 'E'], ['B', 'E']]]}

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt2_PAIR1

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are X and C. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect all paths between X and C.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are X and C.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   Node X is connected to F, K, and G. If I remove the edges (X, F), (X, K), and (X, G), node X becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. This would disconnect X from C. This is a cut set of size 3: `[(X, F), (X, K), (X, G)]`.
    *   Node C is connected to B, O, I, and N. If I remove the edges (B, C), (O, C), (I, C), and (N, C), node C becomes isolated. This is a cut set of size 4.
    Since a cut of size 3 exists, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.

3.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    To prove that the minimum is 3, I need to either find a cut set of size 2 or demonstrate that at least 3 edge-disjoint paths exist between X and C (by Menger's Theorem). Finding edge-disjoint paths visually can be complex and prone to error. Instead, I will systematically test potential cut sets of size 2.

    Let's consider some pairs of edges that might act as bottlenecks:
    *   **Cut Set: [(E, B), (H, I)]**
        *   Path X - F - E - B - C is broken by (E, B).
        *   Path X - G - H - I - C is broken by (H, I).
        *   However, paths like X - G - B - C still exist (using (G, B)).
        *   Also, paths like X - F - E - M - J - P - O - C still exist (using (E, M) and (P, O)).
        *   Therefore, this is not a valid cut set.

    *   **Cut Set: [(B, C), (I, C)]**
        *   All paths ending in B-C are broken.
        *   All paths ending in I-C are broken.
        *   However, paths like X - F - E - M - J - P - O - C still exist (using (O, C)).
        *   Therefore, this is not a valid cut set.

    *   **Cut Set: [(G, B), (H, I)]**
        *   Paths through G-B-C are broken.
        *   Paths through H-I-C are broken.
        *   However, paths like X - F - E - B - C still exist.
        *   Also, paths like X - F - E - M - J - P - O - C still exist.
        *   Therefore, this is not a valid cut set.

    It is difficult to find a cut set of size 2 that disconnects all paths due to the interconnected nature of the graph. This suggests that the minimum cut might be 3.

4.  **Verify a cut set of size 3 (other than isolating X):**
    Let's try to find a cut set of 3 edges that are "closer" to C. Consider the edges that are the final connections to C, or connections to nodes that are direct neighbors of C.
    Consider the cut set: `[(B, C), (I, C), (P, O)]`.
    Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects X from C:
    *   **Edge (B, C) removed:** Any path from X to C that goes through node B and then directly to C is broken.
    *   **Edge (I, C) removed:** Any path from X to C that goes through node I and then directly to C is broken.
    *   **Edge (P, O) removed:** Any path from X to C that goes through node P, then O, then C is broken. This also means that node O cannot be reached from node P anymore.

    Now, let's check for any remaining paths from X to C:
    *   **Paths to C via B or I:** These are directly broken by removing (B, C) and (I, C).
    *   **Paths to C via O:**
        *   A path like X -> ... -> P -> O -> C is broken because (P, O) is removed.
        *   A path like X -> ... -> Y -> O -> C: For this to exist, X must reach Y.
            *   How can X reach Y? Y is connected to N and O.
            *   If X reaches O, then O can reach Y. But how does X reach O? O is connected to C, P, Y. Since (P, O) is removed, O cannot be reached from P. So, O can only be reached from Y. This creates a cycle (O-Y-N-C), but X is not connected to it.
            *   Therefore, no path exists from X to O.
            *   Therefore, no path exists from X to Y.
    *   **Paths to C via N:**
        *   A path like X -> ... -> N -> C: For this to exist, X must reach N.
            *   How can X reach N? N is connected to B, C, Y.
            *   If X reaches B, then B can reach N. But (B, C) is removed, so B cannot reach C.
            *   If X reaches Y, then Y can reach N. But as established above, X cannot reach Y.
            *   Therefore, no path exists from X to N.

    Since all paths to C via B, I, O, and N are broken, X is completely disconnected from C. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

5.  **Conclusion:** I have found a cut set of size 3 (`[(X, F), (X, K), (X, G)]` or `[(B, C), (I, C), (P, O)]`). I have also systematically explored and failed to find a cut set of size 2. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

The final answer is: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, C), (I, C), (P, O)]

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are L and J.
2.  **Understand the goal:** Find the minimum number of non-target nodes to remove to disconnect L and J.
3.  **Initial assessment for a cut set of size 1:** It's clear from the graph's structure that there are multiple independent routes between L and J. Therefore, a single node removal will not suffice. For example, removing F would still leave paths like L-E-B-H-I-J. Removing I would still leave paths like L-F-K-J.
4.  **Explore paths and potential bottlenecks:**
    *   Nodes directly connected to L are {M, E, F}.
    *   Nodes directly connected to J are {D, P, I, K}.
5.  **Consider a cut set of size 2:** Let's try to find two nodes whose removal disconnects L and J.
    *   **Hypothesis: Remove F and B.**
        *   If node F is removed:
            *   All paths starting with L-F-... are immediately severed. (e.g., L-F-K-J)
            *   Node M is connected to L and F. If F is removed, M becomes isolated from the rest of the graph (except L). Therefore, any path starting L-M-... (e.g., L-M-F-K-J) is also severed because M cannot reach J.
            *   At this point, any remaining path from L to J must start with L-E-...
            *   Node E is connected to L, M, and B. Since M is now isolated from J's side (due to F's removal), any path from L-E-... that could reach J must go through B (i.e., L-E-B-...).
        *   Now, if node B is also removed:
            *   All paths starting with L-E-B-... are severed.
        *   Since all paths from L to J must either go through F (or M, which relies on F) or through E-B, removing both F and B will disconnect L from J.
    *   **Verification of {F, B} as a cut set:**
        *   Path L-F-K-J is cut by removing F.
        *   Path L-M-F-G-H-I-J is cut by removing F (as M's only connection to the rest of the graph is F).
        *   Path L-E-B-H-I-J is cut by removing B.
        *   Path L-E-B-C-O-P-J is cut by removing B.
        *   Any path from L must start with L-M, L-E, or L-F.
            *   If F is removed, L-F is cut. M is connected to L and F. So, L-M-F... is cut.
            *   This leaves only paths starting L-E...
            *   E is connected to L, M, B. M is effectively cut off from J's side (as M's only other connection is F, which is removed). So, any path from L-E must go through B.
            *   If B is removed, then L-E-B... is cut.
        *   Therefore, removing {F, B} successfully disconnects L and J.

6.  **Confirm minimum size:** To confirm that 2 is the minimum, we can try to find two vertex-disjoint paths between L and J. If we can find two such paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum vertex cut is at least 2.
    *   **Path 1:** L - F - K - J (Intermediate nodes: F, K)
    *   **Path 2:** L - E - B - H - I - J (Intermediate nodes: E, B, H, I)
    *   These two paths are vertex-disjoint (they do not share any intermediate nodes).
    *   Since we found two vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.
    *   As we have found a cut set of size 2 ({F, B}), this is indeed the minimum.

The final answer is based on the reasoning that removing nodes F and B severs all connections from L to J.

Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [F, B]

**Paths that pass through I (a neighbor of H):**
1.  **E - L - M - J - P - I - H**
2.  **E - F - M - J - P - I - H**

7.  **E - B - N - Y - O - P - I - H**

All these paths rely on node I to reach H. If I is removed, all these paths are broken.

    *   If G and I are removed, H loses all its connections to the rest of the graph (except E, which is the source).
    *   Therefore, E cannot reach H, and they are disconnected.
    *   This confirms that {G, I} is a valid cut set. The size of this cut set is 2.

    *   **If I remove G:** All paths through G are broken. However, paths through I still exist (e.g., E - L - M - J - P - I - H). So, removing G alone is not sufficient.
    *   **If I remove I:** All paths through I are broken. However, paths through G still exist (e.g., E - G - H). So, removing I alone is not sufficient.

        *   Removing B would still leave paths like E-G-H, E-F-G-H, E-L-M-J-P-I-H.
        *   And so on for any other single node. There are always alternative paths through either G or I.

Since removing G alone is not enough, and removing I alone is not enough, and every path from E to H must pass through either G or I, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2. The cut set is [G, I].

    *   **Node K:** Connected to F.

    *   To reach G via X, the path must use the edge (X, G).

5.  **Formulate a strategy for disconnection:**

    *   **Group A:** Paths that go through H to reach G. These paths must use the edge (H, G).

6.  **Propose a cut set based on this strategy:**
    *   To cut all paths in Group A, we can remove the edge (H, G).
    *   To cut all paths in Group B, we can remove the edge (E, F).

7.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   If (H, G) is removed, no path can reach G via H.
    *   If (E, F) is removed, no path can reach F from the "left" side of the graph. Since K and X are only connected to F (from N's side), this means no path can reach K or X, and consequently, no path can reach G via K or X.
    *   Since all paths from N to G must pass through either H or (K/X via F), removing these two edges (H, G) and (E, F) will disconnect N from G.

8.  **Confirm minimality:**
    *   If we remove only (H, G), paths like N-B-E-F-K-G or N-B-E-F-X-G still exist.
    *   If we remove only (E, F), paths like N-B-H-G or N-B-C-I-H-G still exist.
    *   Since removing a single edge is not sufficient, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2. Our proposed cut set has 2 edges, so it is minimal.

A more direct approach is to identify the set of all edges that connect the component containing N to the component containing G.

3.  **Construct the component containing N (let's call it S_N):**

1.  **Identify Target Nodes**: The two black nodes are N and P.
2.  **Explore Paths**: I will systematically identify distinct paths between N and P. To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths, as Menger's theorem states that the minimum vertex cut size is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths.

    Let's find vertex-disjoint paths (excluding N and P themselves):
    *   **Path 1**: N - O - P
        (Nodes involved: O)
    *   **Path 2**: N - C - I - P
        (Nodes involved: C, I. This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, as O is not C or I.)
    *   **Path 3**: N - B - H - J - P
        (Nodes involved: B, H, J. This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (O) and Path 2 (C, I), as B, H, J are distinct from O, C, I.)

3.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size**: Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between N and P, according to Menger's theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect N from P.

4.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3**: Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects N and P. Based on the vertex-disjoint paths identified, removing one node from each path (excluding N and P) should achieve disconnection.
    Let's consider the nodes {O, C, B}. These are the first intermediate nodes on each of the three vertex-disjoint paths from N.

    *   If **O** is removed, Path 1 (N-O-P) is broken.
    *   If **C** is removed, Path 2 (N-C-I-P) is broken.
    *   If **B** is removed, Path 3 (N-B-H-J-P) is broken.

    Let's verify if removing {O, C, B} disconnects N from P by checking all possible paths:
    *   Any path starting N-O-...-P is broken by removing O. (e.g., N-O-P, N-Y-O-P)
    *   Any path starting N-C-...-P is broken by removing C. (e.g., N-C-I-P, N-C-I-J-P)
    *   Any path starting N-B-...-P is broken by removing B. (e.g., N-B-H-I-P, N-B-H-I-J-P, N-B-H-G-F-E-I-P, N-B-H-G-F-E-I-J-P, N-B-H-G-F-E-M-J-P)

5.  **Conclusion**: A cut set of size 3, such as [O, C, B], successfully disconnects N and P. Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, this is the minimum.

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - B

8.  **N - Y - O - P - I - H** (Path via Y, O, P)
9.  **N - Y - O - C - I - H** (Path via Y, O, C)
10. **N - Y - O - C - B - H** (Path via Y, O, C, B)

**Can it be done with 1 edge?**
No. For example, removing (N, B) would cut paths 1, 2, 3, 4, but paths 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10 (which start with N-O or N-Y-O) would still exist. Similarly, removing any single edge would leave other paths intact. Therefore, a single edge is not sufficient.

**Can it be done with 2 edges?**
Let's consider the edges directly connected to N: (N, B), (N, Y), (N, O).
If I remove the edges (N, B) and (N, O):
*   **Paths starting with N - B**: All paths that begin with N - B (e.g., N - B - H, N - B - E - F - X - G - H, N - B - C - I - H, N - B - C - O - P - I - H) are immediately cut because the edge (N, B) is removed.
*   **Paths starting with N - O**: All paths that begin with N - O (e.g., N - O - P - I - H, N - O - C - I - H, N - O - C - B - H) are immediately cut because the edge (N, O) is removed.
*   **Paths starting with N - Y**: The only connection from N to Y is (N, Y). From Y, the only other connection is to O (edge (Y, O)). Since the edge (N, O) has been removed, N cannot reach O directly. Furthermore, N cannot reach O via Y because Y's only path to the rest of the graph (other than N) is through O, and that path is now blocked from N's perspective due to the removal of (N, O). Therefore, all paths starting with N - Y - O - ... (e.g., N - Y - O - P - I - H, N - Y - O - C - I - H, N - Y - O - C - B - H) are also effectively cut.

By removing (N, B) and (N, O), node N becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, except for its connection to Y. However, Y's only other connection is to O, and since (N, O) is removed, Y cannot serve as a bridge to reach H. Thus, N is completely disconnected from H.

This cut set consists of 2 edges, which is the minimum possible since we established that 1 edge is not enough.

*   **Group 1:** Paths that pass through node **E**. (e.g., J -> M -> E -> F)
*   **Group 2:** Paths that pass through node **G**. (e.g., J -> ... -> H -> G -> K -> F)

To disconnect J and F, we must break all paths in both groups.
*   Removing node **E** will break all paths in Group 1.
*   Removing node **G** will break all paths in Group 2.

If we remove only E, paths through G (e.g., J -> I -> H -> G -> K -> F) still exist.
If we remove only G, paths through E (e.g., J -> M -> E -> F) still exist.
Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

Removing both E and G will disconnect J and F. This is a cut set of size 2.

Let's verify that no smaller set exists:
*   As established, removing any single node (E, G, H, K, X, etc.) leaves at least one path intact.
*   For example, removing H would also break all paths in Group 2, so [E, H] is another valid cut set of size 2. However, the problem asks for *a* cut set, and [E, G] is equally valid and minimal.

Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, G]

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of non-black nodes to remove to disconnect E and I.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of the target node I. These are the nodes through which any path to I must pass.
The neighbors of I are: P, C, H.

Next, I will try to find vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. Finding the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths will give me the minimum size of the cut set, according to Menger's theorem.

Let's identify paths from E to I:

1.  **Path 1:** E -> B -> C -> I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}

2.  **Path 2:** E -> M -> J -> P -> I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}

3.  **Path 3:** E -> L -> F -> X -> K -> G -> H -> I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {L, F, X, K, G, H}

Now, let's check if these three paths are vertex-disjoint (meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes, only the start node E and end node I).
*   Intermediate nodes for Path 1: {B, C}
*   Intermediate nodes for Path 2: {M, J, P}
*   Intermediate nodes for Path 3: {L, F, X, K, G, H}

Comparing these sets:
*   Node B is not in {M, J, P} or {L, F, X, K, G, H}.
*   Node C is not in {M, J, P} or {L, F, X, K, G, H}.
*   Node M is not in {B, C} or {L, F, X, K, G, H}.
*   Node J is not in {B, C} or {L, F, X, K, G, H}.
*   Node P is not in {B, C} or {L, F, X, K, G, H}.
*   Node L is not in {B, C} or {M, J, P}.
*   Node F is not in {B, C} or {M, J, P}.
*   Node X is not in {B, C} or {M, J, P}.
*   Node K is not in {B, C} or {M, J, P}.
*   Node G is not in {B, C} or {M, J, P}.
*   Node H is not in {B, C} or {M, J, P}.

Since the sets of intermediate nodes for Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are all disjoint, these are three vertex-disjoint paths between E and I.
According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and I is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. In this case, that number is 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3.
Since P, C, and H are the only nodes directly connected to I, removing all three of them will disconnect I from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. None of P, C, or H are the target nodes (E or I), so they can be removed.

Let's verify that removing [P, C, H] disconnects E and I:
*   If P is removed, all paths through P to I are broken.
*   If C is removed, all paths through C to I are broken.
*   If H is removed, all paths through H to I are broken.
Since P, C, and H are the only neighbors of I, removing them completely isolates I.

Let's verify that no smaller set of nodes can disconnect E and I:
*   **Size 1:**
    *   Removing P: Paths via C and H remain (e.g., E-B-C-I, E-B-H-I).
    *   Removing C: Paths via P and H remain (e.g., E-M-J-P-I, E-B-H-I).
    *   Removing H: Paths via P and C remain (e.g., E-M-J-P-I, E-B-C-I).
    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

*   **Size 2:**
    *   Removing P and C: Paths via H remain (e.g., E-B-H-I).
    *   Removing P and H: Paths via C remain (e.g., E-B-C-I).
    *   Removing C and H: Paths via P remain (e.g., E-M-J-P-I).
    Therefore, removing any two of P, C, H is not sufficient.

Since we have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, and we have identified a cut set of size 3 ([P, C, H]), this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

    Let's list the immediate neighbors of E: L, M, P, B.
    Let's list the immediate neighbors of J: M, D, P, I.

    We can see that M and P are common neighbors, forming direct paths:
    *   Path 1: E - M - J
    *   Path 2: E - P - J

    Other paths from E must go through B:
    *   Paths through B leading to I (which connects to J):
        *   Path 3: E - B - C - I - J
        *   Path 4: E - B - H - I - J
    *   Paths through B leading to P (which connects to J):
        *   Path 5: E - B - C - O - P - J
        *   Path 6: E - B - H - G - F - P - J

2.  **Identify critical "bottleneck" nodes or edges.**
    All paths from E to J must eventually pass through one of the nodes M, P, or I to reach J. Node D is connected to J but does not lead back to E.

    *   To cut Path 1 (E-M-J), we must remove either (E, M) or (M, J).
    *   To cut Path 2 (E-P-J) and Paths 5, 6 (which also use P to reach J), we must remove an edge that disconnects E from P, or P from J. For example, (E, P) or (P, J).
    *   To cut Paths 3, 4 (which use I to reach J), we must remove an edge that disconnects E from I, or I from J. For example, (I, J).

3.  **Consider potential cut sets and their sizes.**

    *   **Option A: Cut all edges directly connected to J that lead to E.**
        The edges connecting J to the rest of the graph (excluding D, which is a dead end for paths from E) are (J, M), (J, P), and (J, I).
        If we remove these three edges:
        *   (J, M) breaks Path 1 (E-M-J).
        *   (J, P) breaks Path 2 (E-P-J), Path 5 (E-B-C-O-P-J), and Path 6 (E-B-H-G-F-P-J).
        *   (J, I) breaks Path 3 (E-B-C-I-J) and Path 4 (E-B-H-I-J).
        All identified paths between E and J are broken. This is a valid cut set of size 3: `[(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)]`.

    *   **Option B: Cut all edges directly connected to E that lead to J.**
        The edges connecting E to the rest of the graph (excluding L, which is a dead end for paths to J) are (E, M), (E, P), and (E, B).
        If we remove these three edges:
        *   (E, M) breaks Path 1 (E-M-J).
        *   (E, P) breaks Path 2 (E-P-J).
        *   (E, B) breaks all paths starting with E-B, which includes Paths 3, 4, 5, and 6.
        All identified paths between E and J are broken. This is a valid cut set of size 3: `[(E, M), (E, P), (E, B)]`.

    *   **Option C: Try to find a cut set of size 2.**
        Let's assume a cut of size 2 exists. This means we can remove two edges to disconnect E and J.
        As established, all paths from E to J must pass through M, P, or I to reach J.
        *   If we remove two edges directly connected to J, for example, (J, M) and (J, P), then paths through I (e.g., E-B-C-I-J) would still exist.
        *   If we remove (J, M) and (J, I), then paths through P (e.g., E-P-J) would still exist.
        *   If we remove (J, P) and (J, I), then paths through M (e.g., E-M-J) would still exist.
        This demonstrates that removing any two edges directly connected to J is insufficient. A similar argument applies to removing two edges directly connected to E.
        More generally, to cut all paths, we need to cut at least one edge from each of the three "bundles" of paths (those going through M, those going through P, and those going through I). Since these bundles are distinct in their final connection to J (M, P, I), it requires at least three edges to ensure all are cut. For example, if we cut (E, M) and (E, P), paths through I (E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J) remain. If we cut (E, M) and (I, J), paths through P (E-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-H-G-F-P-J) remain. If we cut (E, P) and (I, J), path E-M-J remains.
        Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges to remove is 3. One possible cut set is `[(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)]`.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are F and D. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from D.

2.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's Theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them. Therefore, I will search for edge-disjoint paths from F to D.

3.  **Identify Edge-Disjoint Paths:**
    I will systematically find paths from F to D, ensuring that each new path does not share any edges with previously identified paths.

    *   **Path 1:** F - M - J - D
        Edges: `(F, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, D)`

    *   **Path 2:** F - E - B - C - O - D
        Edges: `(F, E)`, `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, O)`, `(O, D)`
        (This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 as it uses entirely different nodes and edges.)

    *   **Path 3:** F - G - H - I - P - D
        Edges: `(F, G)`, `(G, H)`, `(H, I)`, `(I, P)`, `(P, D)`
        (This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2. It uses different initial edges from F, and then proceeds through a distinct set of intermediate nodes and edges.)

    *   **Path 4:** F - L - E - G - B - N - Y - D
        Edges: `(F, L)`, `(L, E)`, `(E, G)`, `(G, B)`, `(B, N)`, `(N, Y)`, `(Y, D)`
        Let's verify this path's disjointness from the previous three:
        *   Compared to Path 1: No common edges.
        *   Compared to Path 2: Path 2 uses `(F, E)`, `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, O)`, `(O, D)`. Path 4 uses `(F, L)`, `(L, E)`, `(E, G)`, `(G, B)`, `(B, N)`, `(N, Y)`, `(Y, D)`. All edges are distinct. For example, while both paths involve nodes E and B, Path 2 uses edge `(E,B)` directly, whereas Path 4 uses `(L,E)`, `(E,G)`, `(G,B)`.
        *   Compared to Path 3: Path 3 uses `(F, G)`, `(G, H)`, `(H, I)`, `(I, P)`, `(P, D)`. Path 4 uses `(F, L)`, `(L, E)`, `(E, G)`, `(G, B)`, `(B, N)`, `(N, Y)`, `(Y, D)`. All edges are distinct. For example, while both paths involve node G, Path 3 uses `(F,G)` and `(G,H)`, whereas Path 4 uses `(E,G)` and `(G,B)`.

    All four identified paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths between F and D, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect F from D is at least 4.

5.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 4:** To confirm that 4 is the minimum, I need to find a set of 4 edges whose removal disconnects F from D. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.
    The edges directly connected to node D are:
    *   `(J, D)`
    *   `(P, D)`
    *   `(O, D)`
    *   `(Y, D)`
    Removing these four edges will completely isolate node D from the rest of the graph, thereby ensuring no path exists between F and D.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 4 edge-disjoint paths, and a cut set of 4 edges that disconnects F from D, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

The final answer is: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D)]

1.  **Identify direct connections from L:** Node L is connected to M and E.
2.  **Identify direct connections to J:** Node J is connected to D, P, I, and K.
3.  **Initial assessment for a cut of size 1:**
    *   If I remove (L, M), a path like L-E-M-F-K-J still exists.
    *   If I remove (L, E), a path like L-M-E-B-H-I-J still exists.
    *   Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect L and J. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Explore potential cut sets of size 2:**

    *   **Option 1: Isolate L.**
        If I remove both edges directly connected to L: (L, M) and (L, E).
        After removing these two edges, L becomes an isolated node, and thus no path can exist between L and J.
        This is a valid cut set of size 2: `[(L, M), (L, E)]`.

    *   **Option 2: Identify critical "bottleneck" edges further into the graph.**
        Let's analyze all possible paths from L to J. Any path starting from L must first go through either M or E.
        *   **Consider paths starting L -> M:**
            *   From M, a path can go to F (M-F-...) or to E (M-E-...).
            *   If the path goes M-F, it will eventually reach J through K (e.g., L-M-F-K-J).
            *   If the path goes M-E, it becomes L-M-E. From E, to reach J, it must go through B (E is connected to L, M, B). So, it must use the edge (E, B). (e.g., L-M-E-B-H-I-J).
            *   Therefore, any path starting L-M must eventually pass through either (M, F) or (E, B) to continue towards J.

        *   **Consider paths starting L -> E:**
            *   From E, a path can go to B (E-B-...) or to M (E-M-...).
            *   If the path goes E-B, it will eventually reach J through H, N, or C (e.g., L-E-B-H-I-J).
            *   If the path goes E-M, it becomes L-E-M. From M, to reach J, it must go through F (M is connected to L, E, F). So, it must use the edge (M, F). (e.g., L-E-M-F-K-J).
            *   Therefore, any path starting L-E must eventually pass through either (E, B) or (M, F) to continue towards J.

        Since all paths from L to J must pass through either the edge (M, F) or the edge (E, B), removing these two edges will disconnect L from J.
        This is a valid cut set of size 2: `[(M, F), (E, B)]`.
